# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Al-Baqarah is from the Madinan period, and it is the longest sūrah of the Book, one twelfth of the whole text. Its name is taken from the cow of verses 67 to 73, the animal the Children of Israel were ordered to sacrifice and about which they asked question after question until the matter was made hard on them. It was the first sūrah to come down at Madinah according to the majority, and the reports preserve that it came down in stages over a long period; the verse about the day on which you are returned to God, verse 281, is reported to have been among the last of the Qurʾān to come down, at Minā during the Farewell Pilgrimage. The sūrah gathers what almost no other sūrah gathers: creed, law, sacred history, cosmology and the inner life, which is why Khālid ibn Maʿdān called it the pavilion of the Qurʾān, and why ʿUmar is reported to have taken twelve years to learn it with its law, and his son ʿAbd Allāh eight. One of the later scholars counted in it a thousand commands, a thousand prohibitions, a thousand rulings and a thousand reports.

Its merits are carried in reports of the highest standing. Muslim records from Abū Umāmah that the Prophet said, "Recite Sūrat al-Baqarah, for holding to it is a blessing, leaving it is a loss, and the sorcerers cannot overcome it." Muslim also records that the Prophet said, "Do not make your houses into graves; Satan flees from the house in which Sūrat al-Baqarah is recited," and the story of Usayd ibn Ḥuḍayr, who recited it at night while his horse grew restless and then saw what appeared to be lamps hanging in the air, and was told that they were the angels drawing near to his recitation, is in al-Bukhārī. The sūrah also carries Āyat al-Kursī, verse 255, and its rank comes in the report of Muslim from Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, where the Prophet asked him which verse of the Book is greatest and he answered with it, and the Prophet struck his chest and said, "Knowledge be pleasant to you, Abū al-Mundhir."

The sūrah opens by naming the Book and describing three kinds of people before it: the people of taqwā who are guided by it, the people who persist in disbelief and are sealed against it, and the people who say they believe and do not. The history of the Children of Israel then runs through the first third — the blessings, the failures, the cow, the covenant at the mountain — and Ibrahim stands in the middle of it as the one who was neither of the two later communities but a ḥanīf, and who raised the Kaʿbah as the house of worship. The direction of prayer is then turned from Jerusalem to Makkah, the fast of Ramadan is legislated, and the long body of the sūrah carries the law: contracts and debt, marriage and divorce, the orphan, war, wine and games of chance, and the final prohibition of usury. The sūrah closes where the Fātiḥah's petition closes — with the words of believers who have been given what they asked for.

---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**THE LETTERS THAT OPEN**

The sūrah opens with three letters, and the Book opens twenty-eight other openings like this one with letters of the alphabet. The early community was not silent about them, and it was not agreed about them. Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and a number of the Companions held that they are among the matters whose knowledge God has kept to Himself, and this report is carried from Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī. Mujāhid said they are the openings of the sūrahs, letters of the alphabet pure and simple, and some of the people of language said God did not cite the whole alphabet of twenty-eight letters, as a man who says, "My son recites alif, bā, tā, thā," meaning the whole alphabet while stopping short. Al-Ṭabarī gathered the faces of the matter: they are the openings of the sūrahs they stand at the head of, they are among the matters God swears by, they are from the letters of the Names and Attributes of God, they are from the letters of the reckoning of the jummāl, and they are emblems and names for their sūrahs.

**THE STORY OF THE RECKONERS**

The reports preserve how the letters were received by those who tried to measure them. Ibn Isḥāq carries the story, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Riʾāb, that Abū Yāsir ibn Akhṭab passed by the Messenger of Allah ﷺ while he was reciting the opening of Sūrat al-Baqarah, Alif Lām Mīm, dhālika al-kitāb, and came to his brother Ḥuyayy ibn Akhṭab among the Jews and told him; and Ḥuyayy walked with that group of Jews to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and they asked whether Jibrīl had brought it to him from God, and when he said yes, Ḥuyayy reckoned: alif is one, lām is thirty, mīm is forty — seventy-one years; and he asked whether there was more, and the Prophet recited alif lām mīm ṣād, then alif lām rā, then alif lām mīm rā, and Ḥuyayy reckoned each one longer than the last, until he said, your affair has become confused to us, until we do not know whether you have been given little or much. His brother Abū Yāsir then gathered all the numbers together and counted seven hundred and thirty-four years. The community received the story as the occasion of the verse about the muḥkam and the mutashābih, the clear and the ambiguous: (3:7 — **“of which some verses are precise—they are the foundation of the Book—while others are elusive”**). What the verse about the ambiguous states, the letters at the head of this sūrah perform: they put the reader on notice that the Book contains what is not given to measurement, and that the right posture toward it is the posture of the ones well-grounded in knowledge in that same verse.

**THE SŪRAH AND ITS PLACE**

The sūrah itself carries its own reports. Al-Qurṭubī states that Sūrat al-Baqarah is Madinan, revealed in different periods, and it is said to be the first sūrah revealed in Madinah — except His saying, fear a Day on which you will be returned to Allah, which is the last verse to come down from the heaven, revealed on the Day of Sacrifice at Minā in the Farewell Pilgrimage, and the verses of usury also being among the last of the Qurʾān to come down. It is called the Fustāṭ of the Qurʾān — its canopy and its city — and the name is carried from Khālid ibn Maʿdān, for its greatness and its splendour and the abundance of its rulings and its admonitions; and ʿUmar learned it with its jurisprudence and what it contains in twelve years, and his son ʿAbd Allāh in eight.

**THE VIRTUE OF THE RECITATION**

The ḥadīth gathers the sūrah's virtue. The Prophet ﷺ said: do not turn your houses into graves, for Shayṭān does not enter the house in which Sūrat al-Baqarah is recited — recorded by al-Tirmidhī, who said, ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said: Shayṭān flees from the house in which Sūrat al-Baqarah is heard — carried by al-Nasāʾī in al-Yawm wa-al-Laylah, and al-Ḥākim in his Mustadrak said its chain is authentic though the two Ṣaḥīḥs did not record it; and al-Dārimī records from Ibn Masʿūd that Shayṭān departs the house in which Sūrat al-Baqarah is recited, and as he leaves he passes gas; and from al-Shaʿbī from Ibn Masʿūd, that whoever recites ten verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah in a night, Shayṭān will not enter his house that night. Al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasāʾī and Ibn Mājah record from Abū Hurayrah that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ sent an expedition of many men and asked each of them what he had memorized of the Qurʾān, and came to one of the youngest of them and asked, what have you memorized, young man? and he said, such-and-such sūrahs, and Sūrat al-Baqarah; and the Prophet said, you have memorized Sūrat al-Baqarah? and he said, yes; and he said, then you are their commander — and one of the chiefs said, by Allah, I did not learn Sūrat al-Baqarah except from fear that I would not be able to implement it. And al-Bukhārī records from Usayd ibn Ḥuḍayr that he recited Sūrat al-Baqarah at night while his horse was tied beside him, and the horse began to start and he stopped, and she stilled, until it happened three times, and he looked up and saw a canopy like a cloud with lamps in it rising to the sky, and the Messenger of Allah ﷺ told him in the morning: that was the angels who came to listen to you, and had you continued, the people would have seen them in the morning.

---

## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**THE BOOK ANNOUNCES ITSELF**

The second verse names what has just begun. The Book renders al-kitāb, and the early reports read it as the Qurʾān itself, carried from Ibn ʿAbbās through al-Suddī, and from Ibn Masʿūd through Murrah al-Hamdānī and other Companions. The word rayb means doubt, and the community of the early scholars is reported to be agreed on it: Abū al-Dardāʾ, Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, Abū Mālik, ʿAṭāʾ, Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, Muqātil ibn Ḥayyān, al-Suddī, Qatādah and Ismāʿīl ibn Abī Khālid all read it so, and Ibn Abī Ḥātim said he knew of no disagreement over the explanation. The meaning is that this Book is without doubt revealed from God. Some of the early scholars also took the verse as carrying a prohibition: do not doubt the Qurʾān. The pointer in this is the demonstrative at its head: this Book, the one in the hand of the reciter, not a book elsewhere.

**THE POINTER AND THE PRESENT**

The demonstrative at the head of the verse has its own argument. The word is dhālika, that, where this, hādhā, might have been expected, and the early scholars faced the question directly. Al-Ṭabarī answers it: whatever has just passed is, by the nearness of its passing, like the present with the one addressed — as a man tells another a report, and the hearer says, inna dhālika wa-Allāhi kamā qulta, or hādhā wa-Allāhi kamā qulta, speaking of it once with the meaning of the absent because it has passed, and once with the meaning of the present because its answer is so close to the speaker's word. It also stands that the Book pointed to is the sūrahs that came down before al-Baqarah in Makkah and Madinah, as if He said, O Muḥammad, what those sūrahs of the Book I have already sent down to you contain is the Book in which there is no doubt. Abū ʿUbaydah and ʿIkrimah held that dhālika is placed in the position of hādhā, and al-Bukhārī records from Maʿmar: dhālika al-kitāb — this Qurʾān; and of the same kind is His saying, and that was Our proof which We gave Ibrāhīm, and those are the verses of Allah which We recite to you in truth, meaning these — but because they had passed, they became as if at a distance, and it was said, tilka. And it was said that the pointer is to the absent that was promised: Allah had promised His Prophet a Book that water would not erase, and al-Qurṭubī carries the ḥadīth of ʿIyāḍ ibn Ḥimār al-Mujāshiʿī in the Ṣaḥīḥ of Muslim: Allah looked at the people of the earth and loathed them, their Arab and their non-Arab, except remnants of the People of the Book, and He said, I have only sent you to test you and to test by you, and I have sent down upon you a Book that water will not wash away — you recite it sleeping and waking. And it was said that when Allah sent down to His Prophet in Makkah, We will cast upon you a weighty word, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ kept stretching his neck toward the fulfilment of the promise, and when it came down, the pointer said: that is the Book you were promised.

**FOR THE GOD-CONSCIOUS**

The Book then names its first audience: guidance for the muttaqīn. The word taqwā is the standing word of the sūrah, and the reports gather around it. Ibn ʿAbbās said the muttaqīn are the believers who avoid associating partners with God and work in His obedience, and he said in another report that they are the ones who fear God's punishment if they abandon the guidance they recognize, and hope in His mercy by believing in what He revealed. Qatādah said they are the ones whom the Qurʾān describes in the verses that follow. Al-Ṭabarī settles the width of the word: the worthiest interpretation of guidance for the muttaqīn is the interpretation of the one who describes the people as those who feared Allah in entering what He forbade them to enter, and so avoided His disobediences, and feared Him in what He commanded them of His obligations, and so obeyed Him in performing them — because Allah described them with taqwā and did not confine their taqwā to some of what He is owed rather than some, and no one may confine the meaning to one aspect of taqwā without a proof that must be submitted to. The structure of the verse is its own argument: the Book is guidance in itself, but its guidance is realized in the one who brings taqwā to it, as a lamp is light in itself but is light only for the one who opens his eyes to it. The same Book is opened with the same claim in the sūrah of the Cave: (18:1 — **“All praise is for Allah Who has revealed the Book to His servant, allowing no crookedness in it”**), and elsewhere the one Book is described as healing for one kind of heart and loss for another: (17:82 — **“We send down the Quran as a healing and mercy for the believers, but it only increases the wrongdoers in loss”**).

---

## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**THE UNSEEN FIRST**

The description of the muttaqīn opens with belief, and al-Ṭabarī carries the word's meaning from its first interpreters: Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, and in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, that they believe means they affirm; and al-Rabīʿ read it as they fear; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said, in the report of Abū al-Aḥwaṣ from him: faith is affirmation. Al-Ṭabarī states the root: faith in the usage of the Arabs is affirmation — the one who affirms a thing with his word is called a believer in it, and the one who affirms his word with his deed is called a believer, and of this is His saying in Yūsuf, you would not believe us even if we were truthful, meaning you would not affirm us; and the fear of Allah enters faith, because faith is the affirmation of the word by the deed; and faith is a comprehensive word for the acknowledgement of Allah and His Books and His Messengers, and for the affirmation of the acknowledgement by the deed.

The unseen is everything that has been veiled from the senses and made known by revelation: God, the angels, the Books, the messengers, the Last Day, and the decree with its good and its bad. Al-Suddī, in the wide report he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ, read the unseen as what is absent from the servants of the affair of the Garden and the Fire and what Allah mentioned in the Qurʾān — and he added the measure of the first community: their affirmation of it was not from any original Book or knowledge they had before. Ibn Masʿūd gave the unseen its rank among the kinds of faith: Saʿīd ibn Manṣūr records from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Yazīd that they were sitting with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd and mentioned the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ and that their deeds were superior to theirs, and ʿAbd Allāh said, the affair of Muḥammad ﷺ was clear for those who saw him; by Him other than whom there is no deity, no person will ever acquire a better type of faith than believing in the unseen — and then he recited the opening of the sūrah, Alif Lām Mīm, dhālika al-kitāb, up to, who believe in the unseen. The verse places the unseen before the seen because the unseen is what makes the seen meaningful. The same two acts, prayer and spending from God's provision, are commanded together elsewhere as the standing business of the believers: (14:31 — **“Tell My believing servants to establish prayer and donate from what We have provided for them—openly and secretly—before the arrival of a Day in which there will be no ransom or friendly connections”**).

**THE TWO PILLARS THAT FOLLOW**

The belief is then carried into two acts, one of the body and one of the wealth. The prayer is not merely performed but established: the word iqāmah means to set the thing upright and complete, so the verse asks for the prayer with its pillars, its times and its presence of heart. Al-Qurṭubī gathers the word ṣalāh's field in the language before the verse takes it up: the prayer is supplication, and it is mercy — from it is Allahumma ṣalli ʿalā Muḥammad — and it is worship, from His saying, their prayer at the House was nothing but whistling and clapping, meaning their worship; and it is the supererogatory prayer, from His saying, command your family with the prayer; and it is glorification, from His saying, were he not of those who glorify; and it is recitation, from His saying, do not recite your prayer too loudly; and it is a house in which prayer is made, as Ibn Fāris said; and it was said that the prayer is a proper name set upon this act of worship, because Allah did not leave any age without a law. The spending is described by its source: from what We have provided for them, so that the giver remembers that what he gives was given to him, and the spending is not generosity from his own store but the return of a provision to its rightful uses. The pairing of prayer and spending is the standing pairing of the Book: the right of God in the body and the right of the servants in the wealth, and the sūrah returns to both of them many times.

---

## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**THE WHOLE CHAIN OF REVELATION**

The belief of the muttaqīn is not in one Book alone but in the whole chain of revelation: what has been revealed to the Prophet and what was revealed before him, the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms and the scrolls of Ibrahim and Mūsā. The verse thus defines the community of the believers as the community of the whole revelation, and it answers in advance the claim that believing in one Book and rejecting another is a position the Book accepts. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: they affirm you with what you have brought from Allah, and what those before you of the messengers brought, and they do not distinguish between them and do not deny what they brought them from their Lord. The early reports read the verse's audience in two ways: al-Suddī's wide chain, from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī from Ibn Masʿūd and from people of the Companions, read it as the believers from the People of the Book, who were believers in the prophets and the Books before, and believed in Muḥammad ﷺ when he came; and it was said that it came down in ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, while the earlier verses came down in the believers of the Arabs; and it was said that both verses are of all the believers together. The reports also preserve the occasion of the refusal: when He sent down, who believe in the unseen, the Jews and the Christians said, we believe in the unseen; and when He sent down, and establish the prayer, they said, we establish the prayer; and when He sent down, and spend of what We have provided them, they said, we spend and give charity; and when He sent down, and who believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you, they recoiled from it. The Qurʾān states the same principle in the sūrah of the Women, where accepting all the messengers and rejecting none is made the condition of the reward: (4:152 — **“As for those who believe in Allah and His messengers—accepting all; rejecting none—He will surely give them their rewards”**).

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as the sūrah's standing argument against the house: the opening of the sūrah, even though its first verses are the description of the believers, is an exposure of the dispraise of the disbelievers of the People of the Book, who claimed to affirm the messengers before Muḥammad while denying him and rejecting what he brought, and with their denial claimed to be guided and that none shall enter the Garden except those who are Jews or Christians — and Allah gave the lie to their claim by stating that this Book is guidance for the people of faith in Muḥammad alone, those who affirm him and what was revealed to him and to those before him, and not for the ones who deny him while claiming to affirm those before him.

**CERTAINTY ABOUT THE LAST DAY**

The verse then closes on the Hereafter with a word stronger than belief: yaqīn, certainty. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the same report of Ibn Isḥāq: that is, the resurrection and the standing and the Garden and the Fire and the reckoning and the Scale — not the ones who claim to believe in what was before you while disbelieving in what has come to you from your Lord. Al-Qurṭubī states the word's measure: al-yaqīn is knowledge without doubt — from it, yaqintu al-amr, ayqantu, istayqantu and tayyaqantu, all of one meaning. The reason the Last Day is given this weight is that it is the fruit of the whole creed: belief in the unseen is tested by belief in a day the senses have not yet seen, and the prayer and the spending are given their direction by it. The Book uses the same word of the same matter in the sūrah of the believers, where the people of the Fire are told that they did not believe and did not pray but denied the Last Day, and in the sūrah of the Spider, where the Hereafter is described as the home of the life that is truly life. The muttaqīn are certain of it before it arrives, which is what makes their worship worship and their spending a trade with God.

---

## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**THE SENTENCE CLOSES THE DESCRIPTION**

The four verses that began with the letters now close on their fruit, and the early scholars read the closing over the two descriptions together. The people of interpretation differed over whom Allah intends by it is they who are guided: some said, the people of both qualities — the believers in the unseen of the Arabs, and the believers in what was revealed to Muḥammad and to those before him of the People of the Book — and He describes them both as being upon guidance from Him and as being the successful, as al-Suddī carried in his wide chain: then He gathered the two groups and said, those are upon guidance from their Lord, and those are the successful; and others said, the verse intends the believers of the People of the Book in particular. Al-Ṭabarī came down for the gathering: ulāʾika points to the two groups together, the muttaqīn and those who believe in what was revealed to you — because Allah praised both groups with their praiseworthy description and made the guidance and the success their common reward. Ibn ʿAbbās gave the two halves their meaning: upon guidance from their Lord, that is, upon light from their Lord and uprightness in what has come to them; and they are the successful, that is, those who attained what they sought and were saved from the evil of what they fled from.

The guidance is attributed to their Lord rather than to the Book, even though the Book has just been named as guidance, because the Book is the means and the Lord is the Giver, and the believer is guided by the Book through the Lord who sent it — and al-Qurṭubī's scholars read the attribution as itself an argument: in His saying from their Lord is a refutation of those who say that the servants create their own faith and their guidance, for if it were as they said, He would have said, from themselves. Success renders falāḥ, and the word carries its field in the language: Abū ʿUbayd states that the root is the splitting — from it is the splitting of the earths for the sowing, which is why the tiller is called a fallāḥ, and the one whose lower lip is split is called aflaḥ — as if the successful one has cut through the difficulties until he attained what he seeks; and it is used for the attaining and the remaining, and of it is the man's word to his wife, istafliḥī bi-amrik, take charge of your affair; and the falāḥ was used of the suḥūr, as in the ḥadīth carried by Abū Dāwūd: the falāḥ almost slipped past us with the Messenger of Allah ﷺ — and the falāḥ was the suḥūr, because the fast's remaining is by it. It is the word the call to prayer uses to summon the believer: come to success.

The structure of the opening is worth reading as a whole. The first verse carries the letters, the second names the Book and its first audience, and the third, fourth and fifth describe that audience in five qualities — belief in the unseen, the prayer, the spending, belief in the whole revelation, and certainty about the Last Day — and then the fifth verse declares that these are the guided and these are the successful. The five qualities are the definition of taqwā from within, and the sūrah will spend its length unfolding what each of them requires. The word success, falāḥ, is the word the Book gives to the ones who respond to the call of God and His Messenger: (24:51 — **“It is they who will ˹truly˺ succeed”**). The verse is also the hinge between the description of the believers and the description of the disbelievers that follows, and the contrast is deliberate. Mujāhid said: four verses at the beginning of Sūrat al-Baqarah describe the believers, two describe the disbelievers, and thirteen describe the hypocrites — and the danger the Madinan community faced was not open disbelief but concealed disbelief.

---

## Verse 2:6

> As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

**THE SECOND KIND OF PEOPLE**

When the believers and their states had been mentioned, the disbelievers and their end are mentioned, because disbelief and faith are the two poles. The word kufr is the contrary of faith, and that is what is intended in the verse; and it may be the denial of the blessing and the kindness, as in the ḥadīth of the eclipse carried by al-Bukhārī: I saw the Fire, and I have not seen a sight more awful than today, and I saw that most of its people were the women; it was said, because of what, O Messenger of Allah? and he said, because of their kufr; it was said, do they disbelieve in Allah? and he said, they disbelieve the husband and they disbelieve the kindness — if you were kind to one of them the whole age, and then she saw one thing from you, she would say, I have never seen any good from you. And the root of kufr in the speech of the Arabs is the covering and the veiling, from which the one who covers the truth and hides it is called a kāfir. The description is shorter than the description of the believers because their state is simpler. The verse states their condition in a single sentence: it is the same whether you warn them or not, they will never believe. The warning is not withdrawn from them — the Prophet is still commanded to warn — but its effect is foreclosed for those whose hearts have been sealed, and the sealing is described in the verse that follows. The verse is addressed to the Prophet, but its audience is the community, which is taught not to measure the success of the message by the acceptance of the sealed. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, that the Messenger of Allah was eager for all the people to believe and follow the guidance he was sent with, and Allah informed him that none would believe except those for whom He had decreed happiness in the first place, and none would stray except those for whom He had decreed that in the first place.

**WHOM THE VERSE NAMES**

The reports read the verse over particular people. Ibn ʿAbbās held that the verse came down about the Jews who were in the districts of Madinah in the time of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, as a rebuke of them for their denial of the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ and their calling him a liar, with their knowledge of him and their recognition that he was the messenger of Allah to them and to people all together. And al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read the two verses — it is the same whether you warn them or not, and Allah has sealed their hearts — as the description of the leaders of the factions: they are the ones Allah mentioned in Ibrāhīm, have you not seen those who exchanged the blessing of Allah for disbelief and made their people settle in the abode of ruin, Gehenna, which they roast in — and they are the ones who were killed on the day of Badr. Al-Ṭabarī gives the reason of this reading: when Allah informed about a people of the disbelievers that they would not believe and that the warning would not profit them, and then there were some of the disbelievers whom the Prophet's warning did profit after this sūrah came down, the verse could only have come down about a particular group of the disbelievers — and the leaders of the factions were undoubtedly of those whom Allah did not profit by the warning, until He killed them at the hands of the believers at Badr; and al-Ḥasan said of the leaders: there is among them none who answers, none who is saved, none who is guided — only Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb and al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ entered Islam of them.

Al-Ṭabarī also states the argument the opening of the sūrah makes on the Prophet's behalf against the rabbis of the Children of Israel who knew his prophethood and denied it: Allah made His Prophet acquainted with what their rabbis concealed and kept secret, which the common of the Jews did not know, so that they might know that the One who acquainted him with that is the One who sent down the Book to Mūsā — for these are matters neither Muḥammad nor his people nor his clan knew before the Criterion came down; and how could confusion be claimed about the truthfulness of an unlettered Prophet who grew up among the unlettered, who does not write and does not read and does not reckon, rising up before rabbis who are reciters and scribes and heads of nations, informing them of the hidden of their faults and the preserved of their knowledge and the concealed of their news — the affair of such a one is not obscure, and his truthfulness is evident.

The same meaning is carried elsewhere in the Book in the identical words, at the opening of the sūrah of Yā Sīn, which begins with the same pairing of the believers and the ones for whom the warning and the non-warning are equal: (36:10 — **“It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe”**). The verse is not a denial of the possibility of belief in general but a description of a particular state, and the state is what the next verse explains.

---

## Verse 2:7

> Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment

**THE SEAL AND THE COVERING**

The verse describes the mechanism of the sealed state, and it first states the barrier itself: Allah has made plain in this verse the barrier that prevents them from faith. The word for seal is khatm, and the early scholars read it as a real sealing: khatamtu al-shayʾ means I covered the thing and secured it, so that nothing enters it — from it is the sealing of the letter and the door, so that what is inside cannot be reached and nothing else can be placed inside. Qatādah said: Shayṭān controlled them when they obeyed him, so Allah sealed their hearts, their hearing and their sight, and they could neither see the guidance nor hear it or comprehend it or understand it. Mujāhid described how the seal comes to be: it occurs when sin resides in the heart and surrounds it from all sides, and this submersion of the heart in sin is the seal — and he graded the degrees of the heart's closing, saying that the stain is lighter than the stamp, and the stamp is lighter than the locks, and the locks are the severest of it all. The people of the meanings observed that Allah described the hearts of the disbelievers with ten descriptions in the Book: the sealing, the stamp, the constriction, the sickness, the stain, the death, the hardness, the turning away, the zeal, and the denial — the denial in His saying, their hearts are denying while they are arrogant, and the zeal in His saying, when the disbelievers placed zeal in their hearts.

The covering over the sight is from another root, ghishāwah, a veil that is laid over the eye from without, where the seal is a closing from within. Al-Ṭabarī states the grammar of the division: the sealing is not described upon the eyes in any of the Book of Allah, nor in any report from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, nor in the language of any of the Arabs, and He said in another sūrah, He sealed his hearing and his heart, and then placed over his sight a covering — so the sight was not brought into the meaning of the sealing, and that is what is known in the speech of the Arabs. Ibn ʿAbbās read the verse accordingly: Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing — and the covering is over their sight. And in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr from Ibn ʿAbbās: sealed from guidance, that they should ever attain it, except by what they denied you of the truth that has come to you from your Lord, until they believe in it even if they believe in everything before you. And al-Suddī's wide chain read it: Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, meaning, they do not understand and they do not hear; and He placed over their sight a covering, meaning, over their eyes, and they do not see. The two images together describe a person who can neither receive the word nor see the sign.

The punishment is then named, and it is described as tremendous, ʿaẓīm, a stronger word than the painful punishment of the hypocrites in the verse that follows, because the open disbeliever's state is more brazen than the hypocrite's. The Book states the same law elsewhere: that the sealing is God's act upon the heart that insists on turning: (30:59 — **“This is how Allah seals the hearts of those unwilling to know ˹the truth˺”**). The verse is thus both a description and a warning: the seal is described as God's, and the turning that earns it is the servant's, and the two statements stand together without one cancelling the other.

---

## Verse 2:8

> And there are some who say, “We believe in Allah and the Last Day,” yet they are not ˹true˺ believers

**THE THIRD KIND, AND THE LONGEST DESCRIPTION**

The third kind of people is introduced with the word some, and the description that follows is the longest of the three, thirteen verses, because the hypocrites are the danger the Madinan community actually lived with. Ibn Jurayj carried from Mujāhid: four verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah came down about the believers, two in the description of the disbelievers, and thirteen about the hypocrites; and Asbāṭ carried from al-Suddī: and of mankind — they are the hypocrites; and Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq: it means the hypocrites of the Aws and the Khazraj, and whoever was upon their affair — and their names were given in this report from Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, which al-Ṭabarī omitted for length. The revelations about the hypocrites came down in Madinah, because there were no hypocrites in Makkah — rather the opposite was the case there, since some people were forced to appear as disbelievers while their hearts concealed faith; and when the Messenger of Allah ﷺ migrated to Madinah, where the Aws and the Khazraj resided, and many of them embraced Islam, while of the three Jewish tribes only a few, like ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, embraced it, the hypocrites appeared in the city. Al-Ṭabarī gives the moment of their appearance: when Allah gathered for His Messenger his affair in the house of his migration, and the word of Allah became manifest in it and Islam spread in its houses, and the Muslims prevailed over the idolaters of the place and the People of the Book were humbled — the rabbis of the Jews showed the Messenger of Allah ﷺ their grudges and their enmity and their hatred, out of envy and oppression, except a few of them whom Allah guided to Islam; and a people of the clans of the Anṣār, who had hardened in their idolatry and their ignorance, matched them secretly in the enmity of the Prophet ﷺ and his Companions, and supported them upon it in concealment, not openly, for fear of death and captivity.

Their mark is the gap between the tongue and the heart: they say we believe in God and the Last Day, and they are not believers. The verse does not deny that they said the words — the words are recorded against them — but that the words carried what they claimed to carry. The community knows them by their marks rather than their claims, and the sūrah of Muḥammad states the same principle, where God says He will show the believers their signs so that they will know them. The ordering of the descriptions carries its own ruling: Allah began with the believers for their nobility and their excellence, then mentioned the disbelievers opposite them, and then mentioned the hypocrites after them and joined them to the disbelievers before them, by the negation of faith from them in His true word, and they are not believers — and in this is a refutation of those who held that faith is the saying of the tongue even without the heart's holding it, for the Book itself joins the word to the heart and refuses one without the other.

The word nifāq, hypocrisy, is from the word for the tunnel of the field mouse, which has two entrances, and the hypocrite is the one who enters the religion from one door and leaves from another; and Ibn Jurayj said of the hypocrite: his deed differs from what he publicizes, and what he conceals differs from what he utters, and his entrance and his presence are not the same as his exit and his absence. The Book states the same judgment on the claim in another sūrah: (63:1 — **“but Allah bears witness that the hypocrites are truly liars”**). The verse is the community's standing warning that the claim of belief is not belief, and that the test is not the tongue.

---

## Verse 2:9

> They seek to deceive Allah and the believers, yet they only deceive themselves, but they fail to perceive it

**THE DECEPTION THAT TURNS AROUND**

The verse states what the hypocrites do with their claim: they seek to deceive God and the believers. The word is mukhādaʿah, a reciprocal form, a deceiver and one deceived with each other, and the verse describes the hypocrite as trying to take the position of the deceiver with God. Al-Qurṭubī's scholars read the attribution in three ways: they deceive Him in their own estimation and their own supposition; or the verse says it because they did the deed of the deceiver; or there is a suppression in the speech — they deceive the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, as al-Ḥasan and others held — and their deception of His messenger is made a deception of Him because he called them by his message, and likewise when they deceive the believers they have deceived Allah. Their deception itself is this: that they showed faith contrary to what they concealed of disbelief, to preserve their blood and their wealth, and they think they have escaped and deceived. And the people of language give the word its root: the origin of the makhdaʿ in the speech of the Arabs is the corruption, as Thaʿlab carried from Ibn al-Aʿrābī — so they deceive Allah, on this reading, means they corrupt their faith and their deeds between themselves and Allah by showing.

The answer of the verse is that the deception returns on the deceiver: they deceive only themselves, and they do not perceive it. Al-Ṭabarī gives the precision of the turning: it is wrong to say that they deceived the believers, because that would establish for them a real deception that passed against the believers, as saying so-and-so killed so-and-so establishes a real killing; but we say, the hypocrites deceived their Lord and the believers, and they deceived none but themselves, as Allah said, not any other — in the likeness of a man who fought another and killed none but himself: you establish his fighting of his companion, and deny his killing of his companion, and establish his killing of himself. And the correct reading of the verse is wa-mā yakhdaʿūna illā anfusahum, they deceive only themselves, because the form of the reciprocal does not establish a deception that has taken effect, while the form of the simple verb establishes it, and there is no doubt that the hypocrite has established the deception of his own soul by what he entered upon of the deception of his Lord and His Messenger and the believers. Al-Qurṭubī gathers the maxim: of their speech is, whoever deceives the one who is not deceived deceives none but himself — and this is sound, because deception is only with the one who does not know the inwards, and the one who knows the inwards, whoever enters with him into deception deceives none but himself; and this shows that the hypocrites did not know Allah, for if they had known Him they would have known that He is not deceived. The Prophet ﷺ gave the rule its form: do not deceive Allah, for whoever deceives Allah, Allah deceives him, and he deceives his own soul if he but knew; they said, O Messenger of Allah, and how does one deceive Allah? and he said, you do what Allah has commanded you with, and seek by it other than Him.

The perception is what is missing; the hypocrite believes he is succeeding at the deception, and the verse says he is failing at it, and the two statements cannot both be true, and the verse is which one is. The Book states the same turning of the deception elsewhere, in the sūrah of the Women, where the hypocrites are described as seeking to deceive God while He is the one deceiving them, and in the language of the ones who plot while God plots. The structure of the self-deception is worth reading: the hypocrite says the words of faith to secure the worldly benefits of belief — safety, inheritance, the standing of the community — and believes he has gained them, while what he has actually done is build the record that will be produced against him on the Day. Qatādah said it plainly: the hypocrites pronounce "there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah" seeking to ensure the sanctity of their blood and their money, all the while concealing disbelief. The Book states the same act in its own words elsewhere: (4:142 — **“Surely the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He outwits them”**). The verse is the first of several in this passage where the hypocrites' own act is described as doing the opposite of what it intends, and the pattern is the sūrah's argument against the whole position.

---

## Verse 2:10

> There is sickness in their hearts, and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase. They will suffer a painful punishment for their lies

**THE SICKNESS AND ITS INCREASE**

The passage now names the state beneath the deception: a sickness in the heart. Al-Qurṭubī states the figure: in their hearts is a sickness is a borrowed expression for the corruption that is in their beliefs — either doubt and hypocrisy, or denial and rejection — and its meaning is that their hearts are sick, being empty of protection and success and care and support; and Ibn Fāris states the word's compass: al-maraḍ is everything by which the person departs from the limit of soundness, of illness, or hypocrisy, or shortcoming in an affair. Ibn ʿAbbās and a number of the Companions read it as doubt and hypocrisy, in the report al-Suddī carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions, and the reports describe it as the disease that prevents the heart from receiving the truth as a healthy heart receives it.

The verse then states God's act toward the sickness: He increases them in it, and the increase is read in the same register as the sickness. Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and Qatādah said that the increase is the increase in doubt; and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam read it as the increase in shameful behaviour, and recited with it the verse of the Repentance, as for those in whose hearts is a sickness, it will add disgrace to their disgrace — evil upon their evil, and deviation upon their deviation. The increase is described as God's act, and the earning of it is the hypocrite's persistence, and the two stand together as they stood in the sealing of the previous passage. The Book states the same law in the sūrah of the Repentance, where a sūrah comes down and the ones with sickness in their hearts are increased by it in defilement upon defilement: (9:125 — **“But as for those with sickness in their hearts, it has increased them only in wickedness upon their wickedness, and they die as disbelievers”**).

**THE PUNISHMENT FOR THE LIES**

The verse closes on the punishment and its reason: they will have a painful punishment for what they lied. Al-Ṭabarī settles the reading of the reason: the correct of the two readings is that the punishment is for the lying that gathers the meaning of the doubt and the denial — because Allah informed of the hypocrites at the head of the news of them in this sūrah that they lie in their claim of faith and their showing of it with their tongues, deceiving Allah and His Messenger and the believers, concealing the doubt and the misgiving; and the other verses witness to it: when the hypocrites come to you, they say, we bear witness that you are the Messenger of Allah, and Allah knows that you are truly His Messenger, and Allah bears witness that the hypocrites are truly liars; they took their oaths as a shield and turned away from the path of Allah. The reason named is thus the lies, and the lies are the words of faith said without the heart behind them, which is the original act of hypocrisy. The reports also carry the question the verse leaves behind it: the scholars differed over the Messenger of Allah's ﷺ forbearance with the hypocrites, not killing them while knowing their hypocrisy, into four sayings — that none knew their state but he, and the judges do not kill by their own knowledge; and that the zanadiqah who conceals disbelief and shows faith is asked to repent and is not killed; and that the one who announces faith and frees himself of every religion other than Islam, his word keeps his blood from being shed. The scholars who said the Prophet ﷺ knew the hypocrites of his time have only the ḥadīth of Ḥudhayfah ibn al-Yamān as their evidence: in it the Prophet ﷺ gave him the names of fourteen hypocrites at the battle of Tabūk, who plotted against him on the hill of the night, and Allah informed the Prophet ﷺ of their plot and he told Ḥudhayfah their names — with the verse of the Repentance beside it, and among the bedouins around you there are hypocrites, and among the people of Madinah there are those who persist in hypocrisy — you do not know them, We know them. The punishment is described as painful, alīm, a weaker word than the tremendous of the open disbelievers, because the hypocrite's claim still gives him a standing in the worldly ruling of the community, and the punishment named here is for the Day. The verse completes the first panel of the three, and the sūrah will now turn, in the verses that follow, to address all three kinds of people together, beginning with the call to worship.

---

## Verse 2:11

> When they are told, “Do not spread corruption in the land,” they reply, “We are only peace-makers!”

**THE FIRST REPLY OF THE HYPOCRITES**

The description of the hypocrites now moves from their inner state to their speech when confronted. The verse records what happens when they are told to stop spreading corruption in the land: they answer that they are only peace-makers. The corruption renders ifsād, and the early scholars read it in its full range. Abū al-ʿĀliyah, through al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and Qatādah, said it is disobedience to God on the earth, because whoever disobeys God on the earth or calls others to disobey Him has spread corruption in it, and peace on the earth and in the heavens is only established through obedience to God. Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd read it as disbelief and acts of disobedience. Ibn Jarīr gathered the meaning: the hypocrites spread corruption by disobeying their Lord, continuing in what is prohibited, abandoning what is obligatory, doubting His religion, lying to the believers by saying the opposite of what their hearts hold, and giving what aid they can to the enemies of God's allies.

The reply of the hypocrites is the second act of corruption added to the first: they do not merely spread corruption, they call it peace. Al-Saʿdī draws out the structure of the claim: they combine acting with corruption and declaring it is not corruption but reform, which is an inversion of the realities, and combines doing falsehood with believing it to be right. He observes that this person is more grievous in guilt than the one who commits sins while believing they are forbidden, for that one is closer to safety and more likely to return. And because their saying we are only peace-makers confines reform to their own side — and implies that the believers are not people of reform — God overturned their claim upon them in the verse that follows. The Book states the same law of corruption elsewhere: (30:41 — **“Corruption has spread on land and sea as a result of what people’s hands have done”**), and the prohibition is stated in the same words at the hand of Shuʿayb: (11:85 — **“nor go about spreading corruption in the land”**).

**WHO THE SPOILERS ARE**

The early reports read the verse over its first people. Al-Suddī, in the wide chain he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ, said: they are the hypocrites, and the corruption is the disbelief and the acting in disobedience. And al-Rabīʿ read the prohibition as, do not disobey in the earth, and said: their corruption was the disobedience of Allah, because whoever disobeys Allah in the earth, or commands disobedience to Him, has corrupted the earth — for the right ordering of the earth and the heaven is by the obedience. Al-Ṭabarī settles the verse's compass: it came down about the hypocrites who were in the time of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, although everyone of their like among the hypocrites after them, to the Day of Resurrection, is intended by it.

The claim of the reform is read in two registers. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: we only want the reform between the two groups, of the believers and the People of the Book; and Mujāhid said, in the report of Ibn Jurayj: when they enter into the disobedience of Allah and it is said to them, do not do such-and-such, they say, we are upon guidance, we are reformers. Al-Ṭabarī reads both together: whichever of the two their claim of the reform was, there is no doubt that they reckoned themselves, in what they did, reformers — they were in all of it, in their own estimation, doers of good, while they were with Allah doers of evil and dissenters from His command; for Allah had made obligatory upon them the enmity of the Jews and the war against them with the Muslims, and the affirmation of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and of what he brought from Allah, as He made it obligatory upon the believers — so their meeting with the Jews upon alliance, and their doubt in the prophethood of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, was the greatest of the corruption, even if it appeared reform in their own eyes.

---

## Verse 2:12

> Indeed, it is they who are the corruptors, but they fail to perceive it

**THE CLAIM RETURNED UPON ITS MAKER**

The verse answers the claim of the hypocrites by returning it upon them with emphasis: indeed, it is they — they and no others — who are the corruptors. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, read the claim they are answering as the hypocrites' saying that they seek to make amends between the believers and the People of the Book, and the verse returns upon that claim that their behaviour, and their claim that it is for peace, is itself corruption, although in their ignorance they do not see it to be corruption. The structure is the same as the return of the deception in verse 9: the act the hypocrite describes as peace is named corruption, and the sentence carries the restrictive particle innamā back against its maker. Al-Saʿdī states the scale of the corruption: there is no greater corruption than disbelieving in the signs of God, turning people from His path, deceiving God and His allies, giving loyalty to the ones who war against God and His Messenger, and then claiming that all of this is reform — is there any corruption beyond this corruption?

The verse closes on their perception: they fail to perceive it. The perception denied them is the useful kind: they may know, in the sense that constitutes an argument against them, that what they do is corruption, but they do not know it in the sense that would make them stop. Al-Saʿdī adds the reason the corruption of disobedience reaches the earth itself: the grains, fruits, trees and plants of the earth are afflicted by calamities whose cause is disobedience, and the true cultivation of the earth is its cultivation with the obedience of God and belief in Him, for which He created the creation and settled them on it and poured out provision upon them. The Book names the hypocrites' end in another place: (4:145 — **“Surely the hypocrites will be in the lowest depths of the Fire—and you will never find for them any helper—”**).

**THE DENIAL AND THE UNNOTICING**

Al-Ṭabarī states the verse's function: this word of Allah is a denial of the hypocrites in their claim — when they are commanded with the obedience of Allah in what He commanded and forbidden from His disobedience in what He forbade, they say, we are only reformers, not spoilers, and we are upon rightness and guidance in what you denied upon us, not stray; and Allah denied them in that of their word and said: no, they are the spoilers, who oppose the command of Allah and transgress His limits and ride His disobedience and leave His obligations — and they do not notice and do not know that they are so — and not the ones who command them with justice of the believers, who forbid them the disobediences of Allah in His earth. The masters of the meanings drew from the form its rule: whoever manifests the claim has lied — do you not see that Allah says, no, they are the spoilers?

The unnoticing is argued over. Ibn Kaysān asked: there is no dispraise upon the one who does not know that he is a spoiler — the dispraise is only when he knows that he is a spoiler and then spoils with knowledge; and he gave the verse its two answers: one, that they were working the corruption secretly and showing the rightness, and they did not notice that their affair would appear with the Prophet ﷺ; and the other, that their corruption was in their own estimation a rightness, and they did not notice that it was corruption, while they had disobeyed Allah and His Messenger in their leaving of the making-plain of the truth and their leaving of following it. And the alliance that made the spoil is named elsewhere in the Book, where taking the disbelievers as allies is itself a corruption of the earth: (8:73 — **“As for the disbelievers, they are guardians of one another. And unless you ˹believers˺ act likewise, there will be great oppression and corruption in the land”**), and the believers are warned against it: (4:144 — **“O believers! Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of the believers. Would you like to give Allah solid proof against yourselves”**).

---

## Verse 2:13

> And when they are told, “Believe as others believe,” they reply, “Will we believe as the fools believe?” Indeed, it is they who are fools, but they do not know

**THE NAME THE HYPOCRITES GIVE THE BELIEVERS**

The third exchange is recorded: when the hypocrites are told to believe as the people believe, they answer with a question of contempt: shall we believe as the fools believe? The people they are told to believe like are the believers, and the early reports name them: Abū al-ʿĀliyah and al-Suddī, in the report from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions, said the hypocrites meant by the fools the Companions of the Messenger of God. Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam said the same. The hypocrites' objection is thus not to the content of the belief but to the people who hold it: they cannot accept to be at the same standing as these people, following the same road, while they — the hypocrites — are the fools.

The word safīh, fool, in the language is the ignorant person of little knowledge of what benefits and what harms, and the majority of the scholars noted that God used the same word of children in the verse about property: (4:5 — **“Do not entrust the incapable ˹among your dependants˺ with your wealth which Allah has made a means of support for you”**). The verse then returns the name upon its giver: indeed, it is they who are the fools. The return is the pattern of the whole passage — the deception returns on the deceiver, the corruption on the corrupter, the foolishness on the fool — and the verse closes on their ignorance of their own state: they do not know. Al-Saʿdī observes that this is more dangerous than the ignorance of the one who knows he is ignorant, for the hypocrite is blind to the degree of his own deviation, and such blindness is further from the truth than the blindness of the one who perceives his condition.

**THE FOOLS AND THE PEOPLE OF CERTAINTY**

The early reports give the verse its first audience. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: when it is said to them, believe as the people believe — that is, affirm as the Companions of Muḥammad affirmed: say that he is a prophet and a messenger, and that what was sent down upon him is truth, and affirm the Hereafter, and that you will be resurrected after death. Al-Ṭabarī gives the definite article of the people its reason: they were some of the people, not all of them, but they were known by their persons to those who were addressed by the verse — believe as the people believe, meaning, the people you know of the people of certainty and affirmation, of Allah and Muḥammad ﷺ and what he brought from Allah and the Last Day; and of the same kind is His saying, those to whom the people said, the people have gathered against you, where the article points to people known to the one addressed.

Al-Ṭabarī states the word the hypocrites gave the believers: al-sufahāʾ is the plural of safīh, as al-ʿulamāʾ is the plural of ʿalīm and al-ḥukamāʾ of ḥakīm, and the safīh is the ignorant, the weak of opinion, the little of knowledge of the places of benefit and harm — for which reason Allah named the women and the children the foolish in His saying, do not give the foolish your wealth which Allah has made for you a standing, and the generality of the people of interpretation said they are the women and the children, for the weakness of their opinions and the little of their knowledge of the places of the interests. And the reports agree on whom the hypocrites meant: al-Suddī's wide chain, and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk from Ibn ʿAbbās, all read the fools as the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ, and in the second report of Ibn ʿAbbās, the believers of the People of the Book — and al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the answer of the verse: no, they are the fools — that is, the ignorant — but they do not know, that is, they do not understand. The hypocrites said this word only in concealment and mockery, and Allah acquainted His Prophet and the believers upon it, and established that the foolishness and the thinness of the minds and the corruption of the insights are in their own quarter and a description of them — and that they do not know, for the stain that is upon their hearts.

---

## Verse 2:14

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But when alone with their evil associates they say, “We are definitely with you; we were only mocking.”

**THE TWO FACES DESCRIBED SIDE BY SIDE**

The verse sets the two assemblies of the hypocrite next to each other. When they meet the believers, they say we believe, and the saying is recorded as their whole religion: a declaration made in the presence of the believers and for their benefit. When they withdraw to their shayāṭīn, they say the opposite. The word shayāṭīn here is read by the early scholars as the devils of mankind: their leaders and masters among the rabbis of the Jews, the hypocrites and the idolators. Ibn Jarīr stated the principle: the shayāṭīn of every creation are the mischievous among them, and there are devils of mankind and devils of jinn, as the Book states: (6:112 — **“And so We have made for every prophet enemies—devilish humans and jinn—whispering to one another with elegant words of deception”**).

What they say in the second assembly carries the whole psychology of the position: we are definitely with you — the emphasis particle inna and the definite pronoun are both present — we were only mocking. The mockery renders istihzāʾ, and the reports read it as the mockery of the Companions: al-Ḍaḥḥāk from Ibn ʿAbbās, and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and Qatādah, read it so. The hypocrite thus lives between two truths, and the verse records both, so that the community knows that the second assembly is the truer report of the man. The verse is also the origin of the ruling that the hypocrites are judged by their outward declaration in this world, since the believers could not know the second assembly, and the knowledge of it belongs to God alone, who now records it against them.

**WHO THEIR DEVILS ARE**

The reports name the devils of the verse. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: there were men of the Jews who, when they met the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ or some of them, said, we are upon your religion; and when they were alone with their companions — and they are their devils — they said, we are with you, we are only mockers. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: when they were alone with their devils of the Jews, who commanded them with the denial and with the dissent from what the Messenger brought, they said, we are with you — that is, we are upon the like of what you are upon — we are only mockers; and in another report from him: we are only mocking the people and playing with them. And al-Suddī's wide chain said: their devils are their heads in disbelief; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said: their brothers of the polytheists. Al-Qurṭubī gathers the disagreement of the commentators over the devils here: Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Suddī said, they are the heads of the disbelief; and al-Kalbī said, they are the devils of the jinn; and a group of the commentators said, they are the soothsayers. He answers the question of the verse's grammar — why khalaw is joined with ilā, when its custom is to be joined with the bāʾ — that khalaw here is in the meaning of they went and they departed. The word of the second assembly is the word that records the truth of the first: the we are with you of the private assembly is the reading of the we believe of the public one, and the verse holds the two assemblies side by side so that the community sees the whole of the hypocrite at once.

---

## Verse 2:15

> Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance

**THE ANSWER OF GOD TO THE MOCKERY**

The verse answers the mockery of the hypocrites with the statement of what God does with it: God mocks at them. The early scholars read the attribution with care. Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and a number of the Companions said the meaning is that God will return their mockery upon them and punish them for it, and Ibn Jarīr stated that God described what He will do to them with the same word they used for what they did to the believers, while the meaning is different: their act is mockery, and His act is the just recompense of it. This is the standing principle of the Book in this passage: the word of the deed and the word of the recompense are one word, and the two realities are not one reality. The Book uses the same pattern elsewhere: (42:40 — **“The reward of an evil deed is its equivalent. But whoever pardons and seeks reconciliation, then their reward is with Allah”**), where the second deed is justice and the first is wrong.

The verse then describes what God does with them in this world: He extends them in their rebellion, leaving them to wander blindly. The word yamudduhum, He extends them, is read by the early scholars as the respite: Mujāhid said He increases them in it, and Ibn Jarīr said the meaning is that He gives them respite and leaves them in their deviation and rebellion. The Book states the same law of the respite: (3:178 — **“Those who disbelieve should not think that living longer is good for them. They are only given more time to increase in sin”**). The wandering blindly renders yaʿmahūn, the wandering of the one who cannot find the road, and it is the fitting end of the one who mocked the road and its people. The scene of the Day completes the image, where the hypocrites will say to the believers, wait for us that we may borrow from your light, and it will be said to them: go back behind you and seek a light.

**HOW THE MOCKERY IS RETURNED**

The early scholars read the attribution of the mockery to Allah in two registers, and both are carried in the reports. A group said: His saying, Allah mocks them, and His saying, they deceive Allah while He deceives them, and His saying, they mocked them and Allah mocked them, and they forgot Allah so He forgot them, and what is like that, is an informing from Allah that He will recompense them with the recompense of the mockery and punish them with the punishment of the deception — and He issued the informing of His recompense of them and His punishment of them in the wording of the informing of their deed upon which they deserved the punishment, though the two meanings differ, as He said, the recompense of an evil is an evil like it, and it is known that the first is an evil from its doer, since it is from him disobedience to Allah, and that the other is justice, since it is from Allah a recompense of the disobedient for the disobedience. Al-Qurṭubī states this as the position of the majority: that is, He takes revenge upon them and punishes them and recompenses them for their mockery, and He named the punishment by the name of the offence — and the Arabs use that much in their speech, of which is the verse of ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm, in which he named his taking-revenge a foolishness, and the foolishness is not boasted of by the one who has sense — and when the Arabs placed a word opposite a word as its answer and its recompense, they mentioned it with the like of its wording even if it differed from it in meaning, and upon that came the Qurʾān and the Sunnah: Allah said, the recompense of an evil is an evil like it, and He said, whoever commits aggression against you, commit aggression against him with the like of what he committed against you — and the recompense is not an evil, and the retaliation is not an aggression, because it is a right that has become obligatory.

And others said: the meaning is that Allah informed of the hypocrites that when they were alone with their insolent ones they said, we are with you upon your religion, and we are only mocking by what we show them of our saying we have affirmed in Muḥammad — meaning, we show them what is in our estimation false, no truth and no guidance — and that is a meaning of the meanings of the mockery; so Allah informed that He mocks them, by showing them, in His rulings in this world, the opposite of what is with Him for them in the Hereafter, as they showed the Prophet ﷺ and the believers in the religion what they were upon the opposite of in their inward. Al-Ṭabarī states the form of it: Allah made for the people of hypocrisy, in this world, by what their tongues showed of the acknowledgement of Allah and His Messenger and what he brought from Allah — which brought them into the reckoning of those whom the name of Islam covers, though they were concealing other than that — the rulings of the Muslims who affirm their acknowledgement with their tongues by the hidden of their hearts and the soundness of their resolves and the praise of their deeds — with the knowledge of Allah of their lying and His acquaintance with the evil of their belief and their doubt in what they claimed with their tongues to affirm — until they supposed in the Hereafter, when they were gathered in the reckoning of those in whose reckoning they were in this world, that they would arrive at their arrival and enter their entrance. And Mujāhid read the word of the verse after it, He extends them, as, He increases them.

---

## Verse 2:16

> They are the ones who trade guidance for misguidance. But this trade is profitless, and they are not ˹rightly˺ guided

**THE TRADE AND ITS LOSS**

The passage now describes the hypocrites' whole affair as a single transaction: they are the ones who purchased misguidance with guidance. The early reports read the purchase in both directions. Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd, in the report of al-Suddī, said it means they pursued misguidance and abandoned guidance. Mujāhid said they believed and then disbelieved. Qatādah said they preferred misguidance to guidance. The verse thus covers the whole range of the hypocrites: the one who never truly believed and the one who believed and then turned, and the Book names the second kind: (63:3 — **“This is because they believed and then abandoned faith. Therefore, their hearts have been sealed, so they do not comprehend”**).

The word of the transaction is shirā, purchase, and the verse uses it with its full commercial force: the hypocrites paid guidance as the price and took misguidance as the goods. The verdict of the verse is then given in commercial terms: their trade was not profitable, and they were not guided. The loss is total: they lost the guidance they gave and gained nothing in exchange, for misguidance has no value. Al-Saʿdī reads the transaction as the summary of the whole passage: the hypocrites are the people of a trade that has no profit, because they exchanged the real for the counterfeit, and the Book states the same exchange elsewhere in the language of preferring blindness: (41:17 — **“As for Thamûd, We showed them guidance, but they preferred blindness over guidance”**). The verse closes on the negation of guidance: they were not guided, in the past tense, because the trade they made foreclosed the guidance from the beginning.

**THE LANGUAGE OF THE TRADE**

The reports give the trade its reading. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: those are they who purchased the misguidance with the guidance — that is, the disbelief for the faith. And al-Suddī's wide chain said: they took the misguidance and left the guidance. And Mujāhid said: they affirmed, then they disbelieved. Al-Ṭabarī weighs the two readings against the context: as for those who said the people were believers and then disbelieved — there is no labour upon them if the affair were as they described, for then they would have left the faith and exchanged the disbelief for it in place of the guidance, and that is the meaning understood of the purchase and the sale; but the indications of the first of the verses, in their descriptions to the last of them, indicate that the people were never lit by the light of faith and never entered into the religion of Islam — do you not hear Allah, from where He began their description until He completed it, describing them only with the showing of the lie by their tongues, by their claim of the affirmation of our Prophet ﷺ and of what he brought, deceiving Allah and His Messenger and the believers in their own estimation, and mocking the believers in their souls, while they concealed other than what they showed? And he came down for the first reading: the worthiest of interpretations is what we carried from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd — they took the misguidance and left the guidance — because every disbeliever in Allah is an exchanger of disbelief for faith, by his acquisition of the disbelief that was found from him in place of the faith he was commanded; and do you not hear Allah say of the one who acquired disbelief in the place of faith in Him and His Messenger, whoever exchanges the disbelief for the faith has strayed from the level of the road? And that is the meaning of the purchase, because every purchaser of a thing only exchanges in the place of what is taken from him another thing in its stead — so likewise the hypocrite and the disbeliever exchanged the misguidance and the hypocrisy for the guidance, and Allah led them astray and stripped from them the light of the guidance, and left them in the darknesses, not seeing.

The words carry their own field. Al-Qurṭubī states the root of the misguidance: it is the bewilderment — and the forgetting is named misguidance, for the bewilderment that is in it, as He said, I did it when I was of the strayed, meaning the forgetting; and the perishing is named misguidance, as He said, when we are lost in the earth. And He attributed the profit to the trade, in His saying, their trade has not profited, upon the custom of the Arabs in their saying, your sale has profited and your bargain has lost, and their saying, a night that stands and a day that fasts — and the meaning is, you profited and you lost in your sale, and you stood in your night and you fasted in your day; so the meaning is, they did not profit in their trade.

---

## Verse 2:17

> Their example is that of someone who kindles a fire, but when it lights up all around them, Allah takes away their light, leaving them in complete darkness—unable to see

**THE FIRST OF THE TWO PARABLES**

The description of the hypocrites now passes from statement to parable, and the first parable is that of the fire. The verse asks the reader to consider their likeness: it is the likeness of one who kindled a fire, and when it illuminated what was around him, God took away their light and left them in darknesses, and they do not see. The kindling is the outward declaration of faith: the hypocrite lights the fire of Islam around himself and gains by its light the safety, the standing and the benefits of the believers. The taking away of the light is the reality of his state: when the light that was not his is taken, he is left in the darknesses, and the verse uses the plural, darknesses, not the singular, because the darknesses of the hypocrite are several — the darkness of disbelief, the darkness of ignorance, the darkness of doubt — and he does not see.

The early scholars read the parable as the whole history of the hypocrites. The fire is the guidance they touched but did not hold, and the light taken away is the faith they declared but did not carry, and the darknesses are the state they are left in at the end. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report Ibn Isḥāq carries through ʿIkrimah and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, read the parable verse by verse: they saw the truth and spoke it, until when they came out by it from the darkness of disbelief they extinguished it with their disbelief and their hypocrisy, and He left them in the darknesses of disbelief, so they do not see guidance and do not stand upon the truth. In another report, from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, Ibn ʿAbbās read the parable as the hypocrites' standing in this life: they were made mighty by Islam, so the Muslims married them, inherited from them and shared the booty with them, and when they died Allah stripped that might from them as the one who lit the fire was stripped of its light, and left them in darknesses — that is, in punishment. The parable is thus a compressed account of the verses that preceded it: the declaration, the deception, the mockery, the trade, all gathered into the image of the man whose fire went out. The sūrah of Light returns to the same image for the ones whose deeds are without light: (24:40 — **“Darkness upon darkness! If one stretches out their hand, they can hardly see it. And whoever Allah does not bless with light will have no light”**). The verse closes on the incapacity: they do not see, which is the same negation with which the passage of the sealed hearts closed.

**THE READING OF THE FIRE**

Al-Suddī, in the wide chain he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ, gives the parable its people: people entered into Islam at the coming of the Prophet ﷺ to Madinah, and then they were hypocrites, and their parable is the parable of a man who was in a darkness and kindled a fire, and it lit for him what was around him, of the dirt and the hurt, and he saw it, until he knew what to guard against; and while he was so, his fire went out, and he came upon not knowing what hurt to guard against. So likewise the hypocrite: he was in the darkness of the association, and he surrendered, and he knew the lawful from the unlawful and the good from the evil; and while he was so, he disbelieved, and he knew not.

Al-Ṭabarī settles the parable's addressee: the worthiest of the interpretations of the verse is what Qatādah and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said, and what ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah carried from Ibn ʿAbbās — that Allah struck this parable for the hypocrites whose description He described and whose story He told from where He began their mention with His saying, and of mankind is the one who says we believe in Allah and the Last Day while they are not believers — not for the ones who declare the disbelief openly; for if the parable were for the one who affirmed with a sound affirmation and then declared the disbelief with a sound declaration, so that the light of the fire were the parable of his faith that was from him upon soundness, and the going of his light were the parable of his apostasy and his declaration of the disbelief upon soundness — there would not have been, in the affair of the people, deception nor mockery nor hypocrisy. So the worthiest of the interpretations is this: the parable of the hypocrites' illumination — by what their tongues showed to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ of the acknowledgement of him, and their saying to him and to the believers, we believe in Allah and His Books and His Messengers and the Last Day, until they were ruled by it, in the immediacy of this world, with the ruling of the Muslims: in the sparing of the blood and the wealth, and the security of the offspring from the captivity, and in the marriage and the inheritance — is like the parable of the illumination of the one who lights the fire with the fire; until, when he had benefited by its brightness and seen what was around him, lit by its light from the darkness — the fire died and went out, and the light went, and the one who had been lit returned into a darkness and a bewilderment. The grammarians read the structure of the verse with it: it was said that the answer of when is suppressed — it went out — and the pronoun of their light is then of the hypocrites, and the informing with this is of a state that will be in the Hereafter, as He said, and there was struck between them a wall that has a gate; and it was said that the answer is He took, and the pronoun returns upon the one who kindled — and upon this saying the likening of the hypocrite to the one who lights is complete, for the remaining of the one who lights in the darknesses, not seeing, is as the remaining of the hypocrite in his bewilderment and his wavering.

---
