Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse's verb by its root: and when you killed a soul — remember, O Children of Israel, when you killed a soul; and the soul that they killed is the soul whose story We mentioned in the interpretation of His saying, and when Mūsā said to his people, indeed God commands you to slaughter a cow. And His saying, and you disputed therein — He means, you differed and contended; and it is only, you contended therein, upon the form of the mutual doing, from the darʾ; and the darʾ is the crookedness; and from it is the saying of Abū al-Najm al-ʿIjlī: the fear of a biter, when they pressed forward, eats the one of the crookedness and sends away the one who is small — and from it, I overtook it before every defender, with the pushing from me of the darʾ of every arrogant. The disputing of the verse is the darʾ of the blood: the pushing of the slain from one quarter to another, and the crookedness of the claim.

And Kathīr carries the readings of the disputing: Mujāhid said, and you disputed therein — disputed about this matter; and ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurasānī and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said the like; and Ibn Jurayj said: some of them said, you killed him, and the others said, no, you killed him; and this is also the interpretation of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam. And Mujāhid said of but God was to bring forth what you were hiding: what you were hiding. The verse joins the two crimes — the killing and the disputing — and the bringing forth of the hidden is the answer of the two together.

---

## Verse 2:73

> So We instructed, “Strike the dead body with a piece of the cow.” This is how ˹easily˺ Allah brings the dead to life, showing you His signs so that you may understand

**THE STRIKING AND THE RISING**

The verse gives the command of the miracle: We said, strike him with a part of it — strike the killed man with a part of the cow. The early scholars read the part as the whole of the miracle: any part of the cow would produce the miracle if they struck him with it, so the command was lightened even at the moment of the miracle. The reports describe the rising: Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-ʿAwfī, that when the dead man was struck with a part of the cow, he stood up and became more alive than he ever was, and he was asked, who killed you? and he said, my nephews killed me, and then he died again — and his nephews said, after Allah took his life away, by Allah, we did not kill him, and they denied the truth while they knew it. The miracle thus did its work in two directions: it named the killer to the people, and it exposed the denial of the killers.

The verse then states the law of the miracle: thus Allah brings the dead to life, and He shows you His signs so that you may understand. The raising of the one killed is the proof of the raising of the all: the One who brought the dead man back with a part of the cow is the One who brings the dead back on the Day, and the sign is shown for the understanding — not for the seeing alone. The Book states the same bringing of the dead to life in the standing signs of the earth: (36:33 — **“There is a sign for them in the dead earth: We give it life, producing grain from it for them to eat”**), and the dead land is the standing cow of the verse: the earth that is struck with the water rises with the grain, as the killed man rose with the striking of the cow.

**THE MEMBER AND THE NAME OF THE KILLER**

The scholars differed over the part with which the killed was struck, of the cow, and what member that was of it. Al-Ṭabarī gathers the reports: some said, the killed was struck with the thigh of the cow. Mujāhid said: he was struck with the thigh of the cow, and he stood up alive, and said, so-and-so killed me; then he returned into his death. And ʿIkrimah said: with her thigh; and when he was struck with her he lived, and said, so-and-so killed me; then he returned to his state. And in the other report of Mujāhid: the man was struck with her thigh, and he stood up alive, and said, so-and-so killed me; then he returned into his death.

And Kathīr states the standing rule of the vagueness: strike him with a part of it — meaning, any part of the cow will produce the miracle, if they struck the dead with it; and we were not told what part of the cow they used, for this matter does not benefit us, neither in the matters of the life nor of the religion — and had it been so, God would have made it plain to us; instead, God made this matter vague, and for this we should leave it vague. And of His saying, thus God brings the dead to life — they struck him with it, and he came back to life; and this verse demonstrates God's ability in the bringing back of the dead to life; and God made this incident a proof against the Jews that the Resurrection shall occur, and ended their disputing and their stubbornness over the dead person. The striking is the verse's whole argument: a part of the dead cow, touched to the dead man, gives the tongue of the name of the killer — and the One who does that with a thigh is the One who does it with all the bodies at once.

---

## Verse 2:74

> Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers; others split, spilling water; while others are humbled in awe of Allah. And Allah is never unaware of what you do

**THE HEARTS HARDER THAN THE STONES**

The verse states what the miracle did not do: then your hearts became hard after that. The hardening is stated after the signs — after the raising of the dead and the proofs that accompanied it — and the measure of the hardness is the stone: your hearts are as the stones, or harder in hardness. The verse then gives the argument of the comparison: the stone, for all its hardness, is softer than their hearts, because the stone does what the heart refuses. Of the stones are those from which rivers gush forth, and of them are those that split and water comes out of them, and of them are those that fall down out of the fear of Allah — the three kinds of the stone are the three responses to the Lord that the hardened heart does not give. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq: some stones are softer than your hearts, for they acknowledge the truth that you are being called to.

The creation that knows its Lord is the standing witness against the heart that does not. It is recorded in the Ṣaḥīḥ that the Prophet ﷺ said of Mount Uḥud: this is a mount that loves us and that we love; and the compassion of the stump of the palm tree for the Prophet ﷺ is confirmed in the authentic narrations; and in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim it is recorded that the Prophet ﷺ said: I know a stone in Makkah that used to greet me with the salām before I was sent, and I know it now. The verse closes on the knowledge: and Allah is not unaware of what you do — the hardening is done before the One who sees it, and the knowledge of the verse is the standing of the warning: the heart that is harder than the stone is being watched by the One whom the stones fear. The Book states the same glorifying of the whole creation: (17:44 — **“There is not a single thing that does not glorify His praises—but you ˹simply˺ cannot comprehend their glorification”**).

**THE OR OF THE COMPARISON AND THE NEPHEWS**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the heart by its hardening: then it — your hearts; he says: then your hearts hardened — after you saw the truth and distinguished it and knew it — from humbling themselves to it and submitting to the duty of the right of God upon you; and your hearts are as the stones, in hardness and dryness and coarseness and severity, or severer in hardness. And if one asks: what is the face of His saying, and it is as the stones, or severer in hardness, while the or, with the people of the Arabic, comes only for the meaning of doubt, and doubt is not permissible in His report? It is said: that is upon other than the face you supposed — it is not a doubt from God in what He reported of it, but a report from Him of their hard hearts, that they — with the servants of God who are their possessors, who denied the truth after they saw the great of the signs of God — as the stones in hardness, or severer than the stones, with them, and with whoever knew their affair. And a group of the people of the Arabic said: God intended by His saying, and it is as the stones, or severer in hardness, and the like of the reports that come with the or, as in His saying, and We sent him to a hundred thousand, or they increase, and His saying, and we or you are upon guidance, or in plain error — the making vague upon the one addressed, while He is knowing which of that was. And they brought the verse of Abū al-Aswad: I love Muḥammad with an intense love — and ʿAbbās, and Ḥamzah, and the entrusted one; and they said: there is no doubt that Abū al-Aswad was not doubting that the love of whom he named is guidance, but he made it vague upon the one he addressed; and it is mentioned of Abū al-Aswad that when he said these verses it was said to him, you have doubted; and he said: no, by God! — then he drew upon the saying of God, and we or you are upon guidance, or in plain error, and said: was the one who reported this doubting of the guider from the straying?

And Ibn ʿAbbās gives the verse its scene, in the report of al-ʿAwfī: when the dead man was struck with a part of the cow, he stood up, and became more alive than he had ever been; and he was asked, who killed you? He said, my nephews killed me; then he died again; and his nephews said, after God took his life away: by God, we did not kill him — and they denied the truth while they knew it. The verse measures the heart against the stones and finds the stones the softer: for of the stones is what the rivers burst out of, and of them is what splits and the water comes out of it, and of them is what falls down for the fear of God — and the heart that does none of these is severer than the stone.

---

## Verse 2:75

> Do you ˹believers still˺ expect them to be true to you, though a group of them would hear the word of Allah then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it

**THE DESPAIR OF THEIR BELIEF**

The address now turns from the history of the house to the community that reads it, and it opens with the question of the hope: do you covet that they will believe in your religion? The question is the despair of the hope: the believers are asked to look at what stands between them and the belief of the house, and the question answers itself with the history that has just been recounted. The verse gives the ground of the despair: a party of them used to hear the word of Allah, then they used to change it knowingly after they had understood it. The party is the party of the house that carried the Book, and the changing is described in its full form: the hearing of the word of Allah, the understanding of it, and then the changing of it with knowledge — the three are the stages of the offence, and the knowledge is what makes the changing what it is.

The early scholars named the party and described the changing. Mujāhid said: those who used to alter it and conceal its truths were their scholars; and Ibn Zayd said, in the report of Ibn Wahb: they altered the Torah that Allah revealed to them, making the lawful unlawful and the prohibited allowed, and the right false and the false right — so when a person seeking the truth came to them with a bribe, they judged his case by the Book of Allah, and when a person came to them seeking to do evil with a bribe, they took out the other, distorted book. The same distortion stands in the sūrah of the Women, in the word of the house itself: (4:46 — **“playing with words and discrediting the faith”**). The verse is the community's standing lesson in the reading of the history: the hope of the belief is measured by the standing of the ones who heard the word of Allah and changed it after they understood it.

**THE HOPE AND THE PARTY**

Al-Ṭabarī fixes the addressees of the hope: do you covet — O Companions of Muḥammad; that is, do you hope, O company of the believers in Muḥammad ﷺ, the affirmers of what he brought you from God, that the Jews of the Children of Israel will believe for you? And He means by His saying, that they believe for you, that they affirm you in what your prophet Muḥammad ﷺ brought you from your Lord. And al-Rabīʿ said: do you covet that they believe for you — he means, the Companions of Muḥammad ﷺ; that they believe for you — he says, do you covet that the Jews believe for you? And Qatādah said: do you covet that they believe for you — he said: they are the Jews.

And the party has its language: as for the party, it is a gathering, like the group, having no single from its own wording; and it is of the form of the doing, from the dispersing, by which the gathering is named, as the gathering is named by the partying, from the partying, and the like of that; and from it is the saying of Aʿshā Banī Thaʿlabah: they were earnest, and when I feared they would disperse into two parties — of them one ascending and one descending.

Kathīr gives the verse its verdict: there was little hope that the Jews who lived in the time of the Prophet ﷺ could have believed — that these people would obey you; they are the deviant sect of the Jews, whose fathers witnessed the clear signs, but their hearts became hard afterwards; and God said next: notwithstanding that a party of them used to hear the word of God, then they used to change it — he means, to distort its meaning — after they understood it; they understood well, yet they used to defy the truth, knowingly, being fully aware of their erroneous interpretations and their corruption. The verse is the sūrah's settling of the hope: the hope is measured not by the wish of the hopers but by the standing of the ones who heard and changed.

---

## Verse 2:76

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But in private they say ˹to each other˺, “Will you disclose to the believers the knowledge Allah has revealed to you, so that they may use it against you before your Lord? Do you not understand?”

**THE TWO FACES AND THE PRIVATE COUNSEL**

The verse describes the two faces of the party that changes. When they meet those who believe, they say: we believe — and the belief of the meeting is the belief of the tongue. Then when they are alone with one another, the counsel changes: do you tell them what Allah has revealed to you, that they may argue with you about it before your Lord? The private counsel is the counsel of the concealment: the knowledge that Allah revealed to them in their Book is the knowledge of this Prophet and his description, and they forbid one another to convey it to the Companions of Muhammad ﷺ, fearing that the Companions would use it against them before their Lord. The verse thus describes the three standings of the party: the belief before the believers, the concealment among themselves, and the fear of the argument before the Lord — and the fear of the argument is the standing that does not produce the belief.

The verse closes on their own question: do you not have sense? The question of the verse returns their question upon them: the ones who ask do you not understand are asked whether they do not understand, and the understanding they are denied is the understanding of what they themselves are doing. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, of their saying we believe when they meet the believers: they believe that Muhammad ﷺ is the Messenger of Allah, but they say he was sent only to the Arabs; and when they meet one another they say, do not convey the news of this Prophet to the Arabs, for you used to ask Allah to grant you victory over them by him, and he was sent to them and not to you. The same two faces are described in the sūrah of the Gathering, in the alliance of the hypocrites with the party of the Book: (59:11 — **“But Allah bears witness that they are truly liars”**). The verse is the standing picture of the belief that is divided between the meeting and the privacy: the tongue that believes before the believers and the counsel that conceals in the private, and the two cannot stand together before the Lord who is the witness of both.

**THE MEETING AND THE PRIVATE**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the two meetings as one character: as for His saying, and when they meet those who believe, they say, we believe — it is a report from God of the ones whose faith He made the Companions of Muḥammad ﷺ despair of, of the Jews of the Children of Israel, of whom a party used to hear the speech of God, then change it after they understood it, while they knew; and they are the ones who, when they meet those who believe in God and His Messenger Muḥammad ﷺ, say, we believe — meaning, we affirmed Muḥammad and what you affirmed, and we acknowledged that; and God informed that they took on the characters of the hypocrites, and walked in their way.

The reports give the two meetings their people. Ibn ʿAbbās said: that was because a group of the Jews, when they met Muḥammad ﷺ, said, we believe; and when some of them were alone with some, they said, do you tell them what God has opened for you? And al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: and when they meet those who believe, they say, we believe — he means, the hypocrites of the Jews, who, when they met the Companions of Muḥammad ﷺ, said, we believe. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: and when they meet those who believe, they say, we believe — that is, in your companion, the Messenger of God ﷺ, but he is to you in particular. And al-Suddī said: and when they meet those who believe, they say, we believe — he said: these are people of the Jews who believed, then they were hypocrites. The verse is the first appearance, in the sūrah's long argument, of the hypocritical character within the People of the Book — the we believe of the public meeting, and the do you tell them of the private.

---

## Verse 2:77

> Do they not know that Allah is aware of what they conceal and what they reveal

**THE KNOWLEDGE THAT GATHERS THE TWO FACES**

The verse answers the two faces of the previous verse with the one knowledge: do they not know that Allah knows what they conceal and what they reveal? The question is the question of the wonder: the party that conceals in private and believes in public is asked whether it does not know that the two standings stand before one knowledge. The concealment of the verse is the concealment of the private counsel — the forbidding of one another to convey the news of the Prophet — and the revealing is the belief of the meeting, and the knowledge of Allah gathers the two as the one act. The verse is the shortest of the passage and the deepest: the whole affair of the two faces is answered in the one sentence, and the sentence is the standing answer of the Book to every division between the apparent and the concealed.

The same knowledge stands in the sūrah of the Mutual Disillusion, in the same two words: (64:4 — **“He knows whatever is in the heavens and the earth. And He knows whatever you conceal and whatever you reveal. For Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart”**). Ibn ʿAbbās read the concealment of the verse in the report of Ibn Isḥāq as the private counsel of the house: what they conceal is what passed between them when they were alone, away from the Companions of Muhammad ﷺ, forbidding one another to convey what Allah had revealed to them in their Book, fearing that the Companions would use it against them before their Lord. The verse closes the affair of the two faces on the knowledge, and the knowledge is the ground of the verses that follow: the ones who do not know the Book but follow wishes, and the ones who write the Book with their hands and say it is from Allah — both stand before the same knowledge that gathers the concealment and the revealing.

**THE KNOWING AND THE QUESTION**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the question over the two assemblies: do they not know that God knows what they conceal and what they reveal — do not these know — the Jews who blame their brothers of their religion, for their being, when they meet those who believe, saying, we believe, and for their informing the believers of what is in their Books of the description of the Messenger of God ﷺ and his sending, and who say to them, do you tell them what God has opened for you, that they may argue with you by it with your Lord — that God is knowing of what they conceal, and hide from the believers, in their private, of their disbelief, and their blaming one another, between them, for their showing of what they showed to the Messenger of God and to the believers in him, of the acknowledgement of Muḥammad ﷺ, and of their saying to them, we believe, and the forbidding of some of them to some?

And Qatādah read the two clauses: do they not know that God knows what they conceal — of their disbelief, and their denial of Muḥammad ﷺ, when some of them are alone with some; and what they reveal — when they meet the Companions of Muḥammad ﷺ, they say, we believe, to please them by that. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: do they not know that God knows what they conceal and what they reveal — he means, what they concealed of their disbelief in Muḥammad ﷺ, and their denial of him, while they find him written with them; and what they reveal — he means, what they revealed when they said to the believers, we believe. The verse puts the knowledge of God over the two assemblies as the roof of the passage: the private counsel was counsel because the knowledge was not supposed to reach it, and the verse states that the knowledge reached it before the words were spoken.

---

## Verse 2:78

> And among them are the illiterate who know nothing about the Scripture except lies, and ˹so˺ they ˹wishfully˺ speculate

**THE UNLETTERED AND THE WISHES**

The verse turns from the scholars of the house who change the Book to its common people who do not know it. Among them are the ummiyyūn — the unlettered — who do not know the Book except wishes, and they do nothing but conjecture. Mujāhid said that the ummiyyūn are among the People of the Book, and Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ, Qatādah, Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī and others said that the ummī is the person who does not write. The unlettered of the house are thus the ones who do not read the Book and do not write it, and their standing with the Book is the standing of the hearsay: they take from their scholars what is given to them, and what is given to them is the changed Book.

The verse describes the knowledge of the unlettered by its two negations: they do not know the Book except amānī, and the amānī are the wishes — the false hopes and the received stories that stand in the place of the revelation. Their knowledge is the conjecture, and the conjecture is the opposite of the knowledge the Book gives: the scholars changed the Book with knowledge, and the unlettered follow the changed Book without knowledge, and the two standings meet in the one loss. The word ummī is the same word that describes the Prophet ﷺ of this Book: he was the unlettered Prophet who neither read nor wrote, as the sūrah of the Spider states: (29:48 — **“You ˹O Prophet˺ could not read any writing ˹even˺ before this ˹revelation˺, nor could you write at all”**), and the Prophet ﷺ said of his community: we are an ummī nation, we do not write and we do not calculate. The verse is the standing warning against the religion of the wishes: the religion that is taken from the hearsay of the changed scholars is the religion of the conjecture, and the conjecture does not stand before the Lord of the truth.

**THE UNLETTERED AND THEIR READING**

Al-Ṭabarī fixes the unlettered their place: and of them are unlettered ones — and of these — the Jews whose story God told in these verses, and whose faith He made the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ despair of, when He said to them, do you covet that they believe for you, while a party of them used to hear the speech of God, then change it after they understood it, and they, when they meet you, say, we believe. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: and of them are unlettered ones — he means, of the Jews; and Mujāhid said: people of the Jews. And from it is the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: we are an unlettered community — we do not write, and we do not count; and one says of it, a man unlettered, manifest of the unletteredness. And Ibrāhīm said: and of them are unlettered ones, they do not know the Book — of them is the one who does not know how to write.

And Kathīr gives the word its field: and of them are unlettered people — meaning, of the People of the Book, as Mujāhid stated; unlettered is the plural of ummī, that is, the person who does not write, as Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ, Qatādah, Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī and others said. And the ummī was one of the descriptions of the Prophet ﷺ, because he was unlettered; for God said, neither did you read any book before it, nor did you write it with your right hand, for then the followers of falsehood might have doubted. The verse divides the house by the knowledge of the Book: the ones who hear and change, and the ones who do not know the Book at all and follow the wishes — and the two stand together in the argument, the changer with his changing, and the unlettered with his conjecture.

---

## Verse 2:79

> So woe to those who distort the Scripture with their own hands then say, “This is from Allah”—seeking a fleeting gain! So woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they have earned

**THE WRITING AND ITS WOES**

The verse pronounces the woe upon the ones who stand behind the changed Book. Woe to those who write the Book with their hands and then say, this is from Allah, to purchase with it a little price. The offence is described in its three stages: the writing of the Book with the hands, the attributing of the writing to Allah, and the selling of the attribution for the little price. The woe is then doubled by the two fruits: woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they earn — the writing is the act and the earning is its harvest, and the woe stands over both. The little price is the standing word of the sūrah for the selling of the truth: the same prohibition was given to the house in the covenant address — do not purchase with My signs a little price — and the verse describes what the house did with the prohibition.

The same offence stands in the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān, with the same three stages: (3:78 — **“There are some among them who distort the Book with their tongues to make you think this ˹distortion˺ is from the Book—but it is not what the Book says. They say, “It is from Allah”—but it is not from Allah. And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly”**). Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-Ḍaḥḥāk, that the woe of the verse is the punishment upon them. The verse is the deepest warning of the sūrah against the changing of the revelation: the one who writes with his hand and says this is from Allah has gathered the three offences of the Book — the forgery, the attribution and the sale — and the three woes of the verse are the three answers of the Lord to the three offences.

**THE WOE AND ITS PLACE**

The reports give the woe its place. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: then woe — he says, the punishment upon them. And Abū ʿIyāḍ said: the woe is what flows of the pus, in the bottom of Gehenna; and in his other report: a cistern in the bottom of Gehenna, in which their pus flows; and in his third: the woe is a valley of pus, in Gehenna. And Shaqīq said: woe — what flows of the pus, in the bottom of Gehenna. And ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān carries, from the Messenger of God ﷺ: the woe is a mountain in the fire.

The writing of the verse is given its three parts by the Book itself: the writing of the book with the hands, and the saying, this is from God, and the purchasing with it of the little price — and the woes of the verse stand over the three: woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they earn. The hand is the instrument, and the attribution is the lie, and the price is the motive — and the verse gathers the three in one sentence, so that the forgery is seen whole: the writing that is not of the Book, the naming that is not of God, and the price that is sold for the names.

---

## Verse 2:80

> ˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, “The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Have you taken a pledge from Allah—for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?”

**THE CLAIM OF THE NUMBERED DAYS**

The verse recounts the house's claim about the Fire and answers it. They said: the Fire will not touch us except for numbered days. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-ʿAwfī, that the Jews said: the Fire will touch us only for forty days; and others added that this was the period during which they worshipped the calf. The claim is the claim of the wish: the Fire is measured by the days of the calf, as if the punishment of the Lord were a debt to be paid in the number of the offence and then lifted. The same claim stands in the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān, with its reading: (3:24 — **“This is because they say, “The Fire will not touch us except for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying”**).

The answer of the verse is given in the command of the Prophet: say, have you taken a covenant from Allah, so that Allah will not break His covenant, or do you say about Allah what you do not know? The answer is the two alternatives that exhaust the claim: either there is a covenant from Allah that the Fire will not touch them except for numbered days — and if there were, Allah would not break His covenant — or there is no covenant, and the claim is the saying about Allah without knowledge. The verse states that the covenant never existed: the claim is the saying about Allah without knowledge, and the saying about Allah without knowledge is the offence that the sūrah has just pronounced the woe upon. The verse is the standing refutation of the religion of the wishes: the hope that is not carried by a covenant from Allah is the lie said about Allah, and the Fire is not measured by the wishes of its people.

**THE COUNTED DAYS AND THEIR NUMBER**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the claim: and they said — the Jews said: the fire will not touch us — meaning, our bodies will not meet the fire, and we will not enter it — except for counted days. And it was said, counted, though its number is not made plain in the sending down, because God informed of them of that while they were knowing the number of the days they appointed for their staying in the fire; and for that the mention of the naming of the number of those days was left, and He named them counted, for what We have described.

And the people of interpretation differed over the amount of the counted days that the Jews appointed. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: that was said by the enemies of God, the Jews; they said: God will not make us enter the fire, except for the dissolving of the oath — the days in which we committed the calf: forty days; and when those days pass from us, the punishment and the oath are cut off from us. And Qatādah said: they said, counted days, by what we committed in the calf. And al-Suddī said: the Jews said: God will make us enter the fire, and we will stay in it forty nights, until when the fire has eaten our sins and cleaned us, a caller will call: bring out every circumcised one of the children of Israel — for that we were commanded to be circumcised; they said: and they will not leave, of us, anyone in the fire, but they will bring him out. The verse answers the counting with the covenant: have you taken a covenant from God, that God will not break His covenant? — and the claim of the days stands exposed as the saying upon God of what they do not know.

---

## Verse 2:81

> But no! Those who commit evil and are engrossed in sin will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**THE LAW OF THE EARNING**

The verse answers the claim of the numbered days with the law of the earning. Nay — the word of the refutation — whoever earns the evil and his sin encompasses him, those are the companions of the Fire, and they will abide in it forever. The law is stated in its two conditions: the earning of the evil and the encompassing of the sin. The earning is the doing of the offence, and the encompassing is the standing in which the sin surrounds its doer as the walls surround the one within them, so that there is no opening of the repentance left in him. The early scholars read the encompassing as the standing of the greatest sin: Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said that his sin has encompassed him refers to the major sins, and the encompassing is the surrounding of the sin that leaves no good deed standing with it.

The verse is the answer of the Lord to the wish of the house: the Fire is not measured by the days of the calf but by the encompassing of the sin, and the one whose sin encompasses him is the companion of the Fire forever. The same law stands in the sūrah of the Women, in the word that answers the wishes of both communities: (4:123 — **“˹Divine grace is˺ neither by your wishes nor those of the People of the Book! Whoever commits evil will be rewarded accordingly”**). The verse closes the affair of the claim on the law: the companionship of the Fire is the fruit of the encompassing of the sin, and the abiding in it is the abiding of the companionship — and the verse that follows states the other companionship.

**THE YES AND THE EARNED EVIL**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as the answer of the claim: His saying, yes, whoever earns an evil — is a denial, from God, of the ones who said, of the Jews, the fire will not touch us except for counted days, and a telling of them that He is the punisher of the one who associated and disbelieved in Him and in His messengers, and whose sins encompassed him, so he is the one made to abide in the fire — for the Garden dwells in it only the people of the belief in Him and in His Messenger, and the people of the obedience to Him, and the ones who stand by His limits. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, through Saʿīd ibn Jubayr or ʿIkrimah: yes, whoever earns an evil, and his sin encompasses him — that is, whoever does the like of your deeds, and disbelieves with the like of what you disbelieved, until his disbelief encompasses the good he has — those are the companions of the fire, they abide in it.

And the yes has its grammar: as for the yes, it is an affirmation in every speech whose beginning is a denial, as the yes is an affirmation in the question that has no denial in it; and its root is the but, which is the returning from the pure denial, in your saying, ʿAmr did not stand, but Zayd; and the yāʾ was added to it so that the stopping upon it would be sound, since the but is not sound for the stopping upon it, being a joining and a returning from the denial; and so that the yes would be a returning from the denial only, and an affirmation of the act that is after the denial. And as for the evil that God mentioned in this place, it is the association with God: Abū Wāʾil said: the association with God; and Mujāhid said: an association. And Kathīr reads the encompassing: and his sin has surrounded him — his polytheism has surrounded him; and he gives the verse its sister in the Book: it will not be in accordance with your desires, nor the desires of the People of the Book — whoever works evil will be recompensed for it, and he will not find, other than God, a protector or a helper; and whoever works of the righteous deeds, of male or female, while he is a believer — those will enter the Garden, and will not be wronged a speck.

---

## Verse 2:82

> And those who believe and do good will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever

**THE OTHER COMPANIONSHIP**

The verse states the other end of the law. And those who believe and do the righteous deeds, those are the companions of the Garden, and they will abide in it forever. The two companionships stand as the two ends of the one law: the companionship of the Fire for the ones whose sin encompassed them, and the companionship of the Garden for the ones who believed and did the righteous deeds. The belief and the deeds are the two conditions of the companionship of the Garden, as the earning and the encompassing were the two conditions of the companionship of the Fire, and the abiding in the Garden is the abiding of the companionship — the same word of the forever, on the other side of the affair.

The verse is the promise that the house replaced with the wish of the numbered days: the reward of the Lord is not the debt of the days but the companionship of the forever, and the companionship is given to the belief and the deeds, not to the names and the wishes. The gathering of the small sins into the great destruction is what Imam Ahmad recorded from Ibn Masʿūd, that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: beware of the belittled sins, for they gather upon a person until they destroy him — and he gave the example of a people who camped on a flat land, and each man went and came back with a stick, and they gathered a heap and kindled a fire and cooked what they cast into it. The companion of the Garden is the one who watches the small as the one who watches the great. The reward is stated with its fullness in the sūrah of the Clear Sign: (98:8 — **“Their reward with their Lord will be Gardens of Eternity, under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever. Allah is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him”**). The verse closes the passage of the claim on its two ends, and the two ends are the standing two ends of the sūrah: the encompassing of the sin and the companionship of the Fire, or the belief and the deeds and the companionship of the Garden.

**THE TWO ABIDINGS**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as the twin of the verse before it: He means by His saying, and those who believe — they affirmed what Muḥammad ﷺ brought; and He means by His saying, and did the righteous deeds, they obeyed God and established His limits, and paid His obligations, and avoided His prohibitions; and He means by His saying, those are the companions of the Garden, they abide in it, the ones who are so are the companions of the Garden, they abide in it — its people, who are its people, abiding in it forever. And these two verses, and the one before them, are a telling from God, to His servants, of the remaining of the fire and the remaining of its people in it, and the remaining of the Garden and the remaining of its people in it, and the permanence of what is prepared in each of the two for its people — a denial, from God, of the ones who said, of the Jews of the Children of Israel, that the fire will not touch them except for counted days, and that they are going, after that, to the Garden; and He informed them of the abiding of their disbelievers in the fire, and the abiding of their believers in the Garden.

Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: and those who believe and do the righteous deeds, those are the companions of the Garden, they abide in it — that is, whoever believes in what you disbelieved in, and acts upon what you left of His religion — for them is the Garden, abiding in it; He informs them that the reward, by the good and by the evil, is permanent upon its people, forever, with no cutting off, ever. And Ibn Zayd read the verse by its people: and those who believe and do the righteous deeds — Muḥammad ﷺ and his Companions; those are the companions of the Garden, they abide in it. The verse is the sūrah's settling of the two abidings over the claim of the counted days: the abiding of the fire and the abiding of the Garden stand together, and both are forever.

---

## Verse 2:83

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from the children of Israel ˹stating˺, “Worship none but Allah; be kind to parents, relatives, orphans and the needy; speak kindly to people; establish prayer; and pay alms-tax.” But you ˹Israelites˺ turned away—except for a few of you—and were indifferent

**THE COVENANT OF THE COMMANDMENTS**

The sūrah now gathers the covenants of the house into their commandments, and it opens with the covenant of the ten. Allah reminded the Children of Israel of the commandments He gave them and the covenants He took from them to abide by them, and how they turned away from all of it knowingly and deliberately. The first of the commandments is the right of the Lord: worship none but Allah, and it is the commandment of every message, as the Book states: (21:25 — **“We never sent a messenger before you ˹O Prophet˺ without revealing to him: “There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Me, so worship Me ˹alone˺.””**). Then come the rights of the creation in their order: the good to the parents, and to the kin, and to the orphans, and to the poor; and the word of the good: speak to the people with the good; and the two acts of the religion: establish the prayer and give the zakāh. The commandments gather the religion into its three standings — the right of the Lord, the rights of the creation, and the acts of the worship — and they are the same standings the sūrah opened with in the description of the muttaqīn.

The order of the commandments is the order of the revelation: the two Ṣaḥīḥs record that Ibn Masʿūd asked the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, which deed is best? and he said: performing the prayer on time; he said, then what? and he said: being kind to one's parents; he said, then what? and he said: jihad in the cause of Allah — the prayer and the kindness to the parents stand in the order the covenant gave them. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī read the good word of the covenant: speaking good to the people means commanding the good and forbidding the evil, and patience and forgiveness, and it includes every good behaviour that Allah is pleased with; and Imam Ahmad narrated that Abū Dharr said that the Prophet ﷺ said: do not belittle anything of the good. The verse then states the answer of the house: then you turned away, except a few of you, and you are the ones who turn away. The turning is stated with its exception: the few who remained upon the covenant stand within the many who turned, and the exception is the standing argument that the covenant was not without its keepers. The same covenant and the same turning stand in the sūrah of the Table: (5:70 — **“Indeed, We took a covenant from the Children of Israel and sent them messengers. Whenever a messenger came to them with what they did not desire, they denied some and killed others”**). The verse is the house's covenant in its gathered form, and the gathering is the argument: the commandments were given in their fullness, and the turning was the answer to the fullness.

**THE READING OF THE COVENANT AND ITS GRAMMAR**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the covenant by its taking: remember also, O company of the Children of Israel, when We took your covenant, that you worship none but God. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, through Saʿīd ibn Jubayr or ʿIkrimah: and when We took the covenant of the Children of Israel — that is, your covenant — that you worship none but God.

The readers differed over the reading of His saying, you shall not worship: some read it with the tāʾ, and some with the yāʾ, and the meaning in that is one. And the reading with the yāʾ and the tāʾ was permitted, and that it be said, you shall not worship, and they shall not worship, while they are absent, because the taking of the covenant is in the meaning of the adjuring; as you say, I adjured your brother, he will surely stand, and you report of him your report of the absent, for his absence from you; and you say, I adjured him, you will surely stand. So likewise His saying, and when We took the covenant of the Children of Israel, you shall not worship, and they shall not worship: the one who read it with the tāʾ read it in the meaning of the address, since the address had been with that; and the one who read it with the yāʾ read it because they were not addressed with that at the time of the report of them. And as for the raising of you shall not worship, it is by the tāʾ that is in you worship, and it is not installed by the an that would have been sound to enter with you shall not worship none but God — because when its entering upon a verb is sound and it is deleted and does not enter, the face of the speech in it is the raising, as God said, say, is it other than God you command me to worship, O ignorant ones? The verse's grammar carries the covenant as both an oath and an address — and the commandments stand in it one after one: the worship of the One, and the good to the parents, and to the kindred, and to the orphans and the poor, and the speaking of good to the people, and the establishing of the prayer, and the giving of the zakāh.

---

## Verse 2:84

> And ˹remember˺ when We took your covenant that you would neither shed each other’s blood nor expel each other from their homes, you gave your pledge and bore witness

**THE COVENANT OF THE BLOOD AND THE HOMES**

The verse recounts the covenant of the house with itself. Remember when We took your covenant: you shall not shed your blood, and you shall not expel one another from your homes. The covenant is the covenant of the brotherhood: the blood of the house is one blood, and the homes of the house are one homes, and the verse names the house as the one self — you shall not shed your blood, you shall not expel yourselves — because the house is the one body in its covenant. The verse then states the taking of the covenant: then you affirmed it, and you bore witness — the affirmation is the acceptance of the covenant, and the bearing of witness is the testimony of the house upon itself that the covenant was taken and accepted.

Ibn ʿAbbās described, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, the standing of the house with this covenant in the city of the Prophet: Allah mentioned what they were doing, and that in the Torah He had prohibited them from shedding one another's blood and required them to free their prisoners; now they were divided in Madinah into two camps — Banū Qaynuqāʿ, the allies of the Khazraj, and al-Naḍīr and Qurayẓah, the allies of the Aws — and when fighting erupted between the Aws and the Khazraj, each Jewish camp fought with its allies against the other, shedding the blood the covenant had forbidden and expelling from the homes the covenant had protected. The verse is thus the covenant that was borne witness to, and the history that bore witness against it — and the verse that follows recounts the contradiction in its full form. The keeping of the covenants is the standing command of the Book: (16:91 — **“Honour Allah’s covenant when you make a pledge, and do not break your oaths after confirming them”**).

**THE BLOOD AND THE ONE BODY**

Al-Ṭabarī answers the verse's question: His saying, and when We took your covenant, you shall not shed your blood, and shall not turn yourselves out of your homes, is, in the meaning and the grammar, the equal of His saying, and when We took the covenant of the Children of Israel, you shall not worship none but God. And if one asks: what is the meaning of His saying, you shall not shed your blood, and shall not turn yourselves out of your homes — was the people killing themselves and turning them out of their homes, and were they forbidden from that? It is said: the affair in that is not as you supposed, but they were forbidden from killing one another; and in the killing of the man of them the man was the killing of the self, since their religion was one, and they were in the place of one man — as the Messenger of God ﷺ said: the believers, in their mutual mercy and their mutual compassion, are in the place of one body: when one part of it complains, the rest of the body calls to it, with the fever and the sleeplessness. And it is possible that the meaning of His saying, you shall not shed your blood, is, the man of you shall not kill the man of you, and he is led by him in retribution, and he is thereby the killer of himself — for he was the one who caused for himself what he deserved the killing by; and the killing of the guardian of the killed one is added to him, by his retribution of his guardian; as it is said to the man who rides an act by which he deserves the punishment, and is punished the punishment: you have earned this upon yourself. And Qatādah read the verse: and when We took your covenant, you shall not shed your blood — that is, some of you shall not kill some; and you shall not turn yourselves out of your homes — and your self, O son of Ādam, is the people of your religion. The verse makes the religion one body: the blood of the brother is the blood of the self, and the house of the brother is the house of the self — and the covenant is taken upon the two together.

---

## Verse 2:85

> But here you are, killing each other and expelling some of your people from their homes, aiding one another in sin and aggression; and when those ˹expelled˺ come to you as captives, you still ransom them—though expelling them was unlawful for you. Do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest? Is there any reward for those who do so among you other than disgrace in this worldly life and being subjected to the harshest punishment on the Day of Judgment? For Allah is never unaware of what you do

**THE BOOK BELIEVED IN PARTS**

The verse describes the contradiction of the house with its covenant. Here you are, killing one another and expelling a party of you from their homes, supporting one another in the sin and the transgression — and Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, that the supporting was toward the people of idolatry, until they shed their blood with them, and Ibn Isḥāq said that this passage was revealed about what they did with the Aws and the Khazraj. And if they come to you as captives, you ransom them, while their expulsion was forbidden to you. The contradiction is described in its two halves: the house sheds the blood and expels from the homes where the covenant forbade the blood and the expulsion, and the house ransoms the captives where the covenant commanded the ransom — the same house breaks the covenant in its first half and keeps it in its second. The verse then asks the question of the contradiction: do you believe in a part of the Book and disbelieve in a part? The belief in the Book is not divided: the one who keeps the ransom and breaks the blood has not kept the Book — he has chosen from it, and the choosing from the Book is the disbelief in the part he left.

The verse then states the reward of the division: what is the reward of the one who does that among you, except the ignominy in the life of this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they are returned to the severest punishment? The two rewards are the two ends of the one act: the ignominy of this world and the severest punishment of the next, and the verse closes on the knowledge: and Allah is not unaware of what you do. The same dividing of the Book stands in the sūrah of the Women, in the description of the ones who divide between Allah and His messengers: (4:150 — **“Surely those who deny Allah and His messengers and wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, saying, “We believe in some and disbelieve in others,” desiring to forge a compromise”**). The verse is the sūrah's standing verdict on the religion of the selection: the Book is taken whole or it is not taken, and the one who believes in a part and disbelieves in a part has disbelieved in the whole.

**THE THEN AND THE TWO FACES OF THE ADDRESS**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the opening of the verse by its two faces: two faces are possible in His saying, then you, these: one of them is that it is intended by it, then you, O these — and the yāʾ is left, by the sufficiency of the indication of the speech upon it, as He said, Yūsuf, turn away from this, and its interpretation is, O Yūsuf, turn away from this; and the meaning of the speech then is: then you, O company of the Jews of the Children of Israel — after your affirmation of the covenant I took upon you, you shall not shed your blood, and shall not turn yourselves out of your homes, then you affirmed, after your witnessing upon yourselves — you kill yourselves and turn out a party of you from their homes, helping one another against them, in the sin and the aggression. And the cooperation is the helping one another; and the cooperation was named the backing one another only for the strengthening of some of them the back of some; and it is of the form of the mutual doing, from the back, and it is the supporting of some of them his back to the back of some. And the other face is that its meaning is, then you are a people who kill yourselves — and it returns to the report about the you; and these intervened between them and between the report of them, as the Arabs say, I am this, I stand, and I am this, I sit.

And Kathīr gives the verse its history: God criticized the Jews who lived in Madinah in the time of the Messenger of God ﷺ; they used to suffer, because of the armed conflicts between the tribes of Madinah, Aws and Khazraj; and there were three Jewish tribes in Madinah at that time — Banū Qaynuqāʿ and Banū al-Naḍīr, the allies of the Khazraj, and Banū Qurayẓah, who were the allies of the Aws. The verse names the contradiction by its scene: the fighting with the ally against the brother, and the ransoming of the same brother when he comes as a captive — and the expulsion that was forbidden is done by the hands, and the ransoming that was commanded is done by the wealth.

---

## Verse 2:86

> These are the ones who trade the Hereafter for the life of this world. So their punishment will not be reduced, nor will they be helped

**THE TRADE AND ITS PRICE**

Ibn Isḥāq reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that Allah was describing what the house was doing in Madinah: in the Torah He had prohibited them from shedding one another's blood and required them to free their prisoners, and they had divided into the camps of the Aws and the Khazraj — and on that standing the verse of the trade descends. Those are they who have purchased the life of this world at the price of the Hereafter. The verse describes the division of the Book as a trade: the house that kept the ransom and broke the blood did not merely break a commandment — it made a bargain, and the bargain was the taking of the life of this world in exchange for the Hereafter. The ransom was kept for the price it brought in the world, and the blood was shed for the standing it bought in the world, and the Hereafter was the price that was paid for both. The verse thus reads the contradiction of the previous verse with the reading of the trade: the one who believes in a part and disbelieves in a part is the one who has sold the whole for the part, and the part he kept is the life of this world.

The verse then states the two negations of the trade: so their punishment will not be lightened, and they will not be helped. The lightening is refused to the punishment and the helping is refused to the punished, and the two negations are the two fruits of the trade: the one who purchased this world at the price of the Hereafter will find no lightening in the punishment he purchased it with, and no helper on the Day he sold it for. The Book states the root of the trade elsewhere: (16:107 — **“This is because they prefer the life of this world over the Hereafter. Surely Allah never guides those who ˹choose to˺ disbelieve”**). The verse closes the affair of the division of the Book on the two ends of the trade: the price that was paid — the Hereafter — and the standing that remains — the punishment that is not lightened and the help that does not come.

**THE PURCHASE AND ITS PRICE**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the purchase by its two sides: those are the ones who were reported of that they believe in part of the Book — so they ransom their captives of the Jews — and disbelieve in part — so they kill, of the people of their religion, the one God forbade them the killing of, and they turn out of his home the one God forbade them the turning out of, in breaking of the covenant and the pact of God, in the Torah, to them. And God informed that these are the ones who purchased the leadership of the life of this world, over the weak and the people of ignorance and stupidity of the people of their religion, and bought the mean and base earnings in it, with the faith. And Qatādah said: those are the ones who purchased the life of this world for the Hereafter — they preferred the little of this world over the much of the Hereafter.

Then God informed that when they sold their portions of the bliss of the Hereafter — by their leaving of His obedience, and their preferring the disbelief in Him and the mean of this world over it — they have no portion in the bliss of the Hereafter, and that what is theirs in the Hereafter is the punishment, not lightened to them in it — for the one for whom the punishment is lightened in it is the one who has a portion of its bliss, and these have no portion, for their purchasing, by what was in this world, their world with their Hereafter. And as for His saying, and they will not be helped — He informed of them that none will help them in the Hereafter, so that he repels from them, by his help, the punishment of God — neither by his strength, nor by his intercession, nor by other than that. The verse is the sūrah's own weighing of the partial belief: the ransoming is done by the Book, and the killing is done against the Book, and the purchase of the verse gathers the two — and the price is the life of this world.

---

## Verse 2:87

> Indeed, We gave Moses the Book and sent after him successive messengers. And We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit. Why is it that every time a messenger comes to you ˹Israelites˺ with something you do not like, you become arrogant, rejecting some and killing others

**THE SUCCESSION AND THE ARROGANCE**

The verse recounts the house's standing with the succession of the messengers. Allah describes the insolence of the Children of Israel and their rebelliousness and arrogance toward the prophets, and their following of their lusts and desires. The verse begins with the gifts: We gave Mūsā the Book, and We followed him with the messengers, one after another. Al-Suddī said, from Abū Mālik, that qaffaynā means We sent in succession, and others read it as We followed. The succession was the standing care of the Lord over the house: the Book was given at the head of it, and the messengers followed one after another carrying its renewal, until the last of the prophets of the Children of Israel, and after him the Prophet of this Book. The verse names the greatest of the succession: ʿĪsā, the son of Maryam, whom Allah gave the clear signs and supported with Rūḥ al-Qudus. Ibn Masʿūd stated, in explanation of this verse, that Rūḥ al-Qudus is Jibrīl, and this is also the view of Ibn ʿAbbās, Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb, Ismāʿīl ibn Khālid, al-Suddī, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, ʿAṭiyyah al-ʿAwfī and Qatādah. The same standing of ʿĪsā stands in the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān: (5:46 — **“Then in the footsteps of the prophets, We sent Jesus, son of Mary, confirming the Torah revealed before him. And We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light”**).

The verse then turns from the gifts to the answer they received: is it that whenever a messenger comes to you with what your souls desire not, you grow arrogant, and some you disbelieve and some you kill? The question is the wonder of the Lord over the house: the messengers came with the Book and after the Book, and the house measured them by its desires, and what crossed the desire was answered with the arrogance, and the arrogance ended in the disbelief of some and the killing of some. The verse is the standing verdict on the religion that measures the messengers by the soul: the one who takes his desire as the judge of the revelation will end by disbelieving some of the messengers and killing some, because the revelation will always come with what the soul desires not.

**THE FOLLOWING AND THE CLEAR SIGNS**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse's two gifts: He means by His saying, We gave Mūsā the Book, We sent it down to him. And as for His saying, and We followed — He means, We made some to follow some, and We made some follow some, one behind another, as the man follows the man, when he walks in his track, behind him; and its root is from the nape; one says of it, I followed so-and-so, when I came behind his nape, as one says, I came after him, when I came after him. And He means by the messengers the prophets. And He intends by His saying, and We followed after him with the messengers, We made some of them follow some, upon one way and one law — for every one whom God sent as a prophet after Mūsā, until the time of ʿĪsā the son of Maryam, He sent him commanding the Children of Israel with the establishing of the Torah, and the acting upon what is in it, and the calling to what is in it; and for that it was said, and We followed after him with the messengers — meaning, upon his way and his law, and the acting upon what he used to act upon. And He means by the clear proofs, what He showed upon his hands, of the arguments and the indication of his prophethood: of the bringing to life of the dead, and the healing of the one born blind, and the like of those signs, which made plain his station with God, and indicated his truth and the soundness of his prophethood.

And Kathīr states the verse's verdict: God described the insolence of the Children of Israel, and their rebelliousness and defiance and arrogance toward the prophets, following their lusts and desires; and He mentioned that He gave Mūsā the Book, the Torah, and that the Jews changed and distorted and defied its commands, and altered its meanings; and God sent messengers and prophets after Mūsā, who followed his law. The verse's question gathers the whole of the story: is it that, whenever there came to you a messenger with what your souls desired not, you grew arrogant, some you disbelieved, and some you killed?

---

## Verse 2:88

> They say, “Our hearts are unreceptive!” In fact, Allah has condemned them for their disbelief. They have but little faith

**THE HEARTS AND THE CURSE**

The verse recounts the house's excuse for the disbelief. They say: our hearts are ghulf. Ibn Isḥāq reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that it means our hearts are screened; and Mujāhid said they are covered; ʿIkrimah said there is a stamp upon them; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah said they do not comprehend. The house claims that the hearts are wrapped in their coverings, and that the revelation cannot enter them, and the claim is made an excuse for the refusal: the hearts are not at fault, the fault is in the covering. Mujāhid and Qatādah reported another reading from Ibn ʿAbbās, and it is the opinion of ʿAṭāʾ: our hearts contain every type of knowledge, and they do not need the knowledge that you have — and on this reading the claim is the claim of the sufficiency, not the claim of the incapacity.

The verse answers the claim with the curse: nay, but Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so little is what they believe. The curse is the answer of the Lord to the excuse of the hearts: the covering that the house blames is not the cause of the disbelief but its fruit — the hearts were cursed for the disbelief that preceded the covering, and the covering was the seal upon the disbelief, not the excuse for it. The same saying of the house and its answer stand in the sūrah of the Women, among the curses of the house: (4:155 — **“and for saying, “Our hearts are unreceptive!”—it is Allah Who has sealed their hearts for their disbelief, so they do not believe except for a few”**). The verse closes on the measure of their belief: little is what they believe — the belief that remains with the house is the belief of the tongue and the name, and the curse stands over the little that remains.

**THE COVERED HEARTS AND THEIR READING**

The readers read the word of the hearts in two ways. Al-Ṭabarī states them: some read it, and they say, our hearts are covered, with the lām lightened and still — and it is the reading of the generality of the towns, in all the horizons; and some read it, our hearts are coverings, with the lām heavy and raised. And as for those who read it with the stillness of the lām and its lightening, they interpreted it that they said: our hearts are in sheaths and coverings and covering; and the covered, upon the reading of these, is the plural of the covered one, and it is the one that is in a sheath and a covering, as it is said of the man who is not circumcised, uncircumcised, and of the woman, uncircumcised; and as it is said of the sword, when it is in its sheath, a sheathed sword, and a sheathed bow; and its plural is covered, and so is gathered what was of the adjectives whose masculine is upon the form of the doer and whose feminine is upon the form of the done, gathered upon the form of the plural.

And Ḥudhayfah gives the hearts their numbering: the hearts are four — and he mentioned them — and he said, of what he mentioned: and a heart covered, bound upon it — and that is the heart of the disbeliever. The verse answers the covering with the curse: nay, God has cursed them for their disbelief — and the covering they claimed as an excuse is named the effect of the curse, not its cause. The little of the belief that remains is measured by the curse that came before it: the heart that is covered by the curse does not receive, and the verse states the order of the two — the disbelief first, and the covering after.

---

## Verse 2:89

> Although they used to pray for victory ˹by means of the Prophet˺ over the polytheists, when there came to them a Book from Allah which they recognized, confirming the Scripture they had ˹in their hands˺, they rejected it. So may Allah’s condemnation be upon the disbelievers

**THE VICTORY THEY INVOKED**

The verse recounts the deepest of the house's contradictions: the invocation before the sending, and the disbelief after it. Before the Messenger came to them, they used to invoke Allah for victory by his coming over the disbelievers. The reports describe the invocation: they used to say to the polytheists, a prophet shall be sent just before the end of this world, and we, along with him, shall exterminate you, just as the nations of ʿĀd and Iram were exterminated. Ibn Isḥāq narrated from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Jews used to invoke Allah for the coming of Muhammad ﷺ to gain victory over the Aws and the Khazraj before the Prophet was sent; and when Allah sent him to the Arabs, they rejected him and denied what they used to say about him. The house thus invoked the coming of the Prophet as its victory, and when the Prophet came, the victory was given to the ones the house fought, and the house disbelieved in the one it had invoked.

The verse then states the coming of the Book: when there came to them a Book from Allah — the Qurʾān that Allah sent down to Muhammad ﷺ — confirming what was with them, meaning the Torah. The confirming is the proof of the house upon itself: the Book that came is the Book that their Book described, and they knew it as they knew their own sons, and the knowledge is what the curse of the verse is pronounced upon: so the curse of Allah is upon the disbelievers. The verse is the standing verdict on the one who invokes the truth and then refuses it when it comes: the house that asked Allah for victory by the Prophet was answered by the Prophet, and its answer was the disbelief of the one who asked — and the curse of the verse is the closing of the affair. The same recognition stands in the verse the sūrah will come to: (2:146 — **“Those We have given the Scripture recognize this ˹Prophet˺ as they recognize their own children. Yet a group of them hides the truth knowingly”**).

**THE SEEKING OF THE VICTORY AND THE RECOGNIZING**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse by its two clauses: and when there came to them a Book from God, confirming what was with them — when there came to the Jews of the Children of Israel whom God described the description of, a Book from God — He means by the Book the Qurʾān that God sent down upon Muḥammad ﷺ — confirming what was with them, meaning, confirming what was with them of the Books that God sent down before the Qurʾān. And Qatādah said: and when there came to them a Book from God, confirming what was with them — and it is the Qurʾān that was sent down upon Muḥammad, confirming what was with them of the Torah and the Gospel; and al-Rabīʿ said the like.

And of His saying, and they used, before that, to seek the victory over those who disbelieved — al-Ṭabarī reads it: and these Jews — who, when there came to them a Book from God, confirming what was with them of the Books, disbelieved in it — used to seek the victory by Muḥammad ﷺ; and the meaning of the seeking of the victory is the seeking of the help; they used to seek the help of God by him, over the associators of the Arabs, before his sending — that is, before he was sent. And Kathīr gives the seeking its word: before this Messenger came to them, they used to ask God to aid them by his arrival, against their polytheistic enemies in war; and they used to say to the polytheists, a prophet shall be sent just before the end of this world, and we, along with him, shall exterminate you, just as the nations of ʿĀd and Iram were exterminated. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: the Jews used to ask God to send Muḥammad ﷺ, so that they would gain victory over the Arab disbelievers; and they used to say, O God, send the prophet that we read of, in the Torah, so that we can torment and kill the disbelievers alongside him; and when God sent Muḥammad ﷺ, and they saw that he was not one of them, they rejected him and envied the Arabs, even though they knew that he was the Messenger of God. The verse is the story of the recognition turned into its opposite: the one they used to seek the victory by is the one they disbelieved in, when the recognition came with a face they desired not.

---

## Verse 2:90

> Miserable is the price they have sold their souls for—denying Allah’s revelation and resenting Allah for granting His grace to whoever He wills of His servants! They have earned wrath upon wrath. And such disbelievers will suffer a humiliating punishment

**THE ENVY AND THE DOUBLE WRATH**

The verse reads the price of the trade. Wretched is that for which they sold their souls: that they disbelieve in what Allah has sent down, grudging that Allah should send down of His grace upon whom He wills of His servants. The verse gives the two halves of the trade: the disbelief in what Allah sent down, and the grudging of the grace of Allah. Mujāhid said: the Jews sold the truth for falsehood and hid the truth about Muhammad ﷺ; and al-Suddī said: the Jews sold themselves, meaning that what is wretched is what they chose for themselves by disbelieving in what Allah revealed to Muhammad ﷺ instead of believing in him and aiding him and supporting him — and their behaviour is the fruit of their injustice, their envy and their hatred. The root of the trade is the grudging: the grace of Allah was sent down upon a Prophet from the Arabs, and the house grudged Him His grace, and the grudging of the grace is the disbelief in the Giver of the grace.

The verse then states the fruit of the trade: they returned with wrath upon wrath. The double wrath is read by the early scholars as the wrath upon the disbelief and the wrath upon the grudging, or as the wrath of the calf and the wrath of this rejection, and the doubling is the standing of the one who adds a new disbelief to an old one. The same wretched price returns at the end of the sūrah's narrative of the magic: (2:102 — **“Miserable indeed was the price for which they sold their souls, if only they knew”**). The verse closes on the punishment: and for the disbelievers there is a humiliating punishment, and the humiliation of the punishment is the answer to the arrogance of the disbelieving. The verse is the sūrah's deepest reading of the house's refusal: the refusal was not a doubt of the truth but an envy of the grace, and the envy of the grace of Allah is the disbelief in Allah — and the double wrath is the measure of the double offence.

**THE EVIL OF THE SALE AND THE ROOT OF THE ENVY**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the word of the sale: the meaning of His saying, evil is what they purchased their souls with, is, evil is what they purchased their souls with; and the root of evil is miserable, of the misery; its hamza was stilled, then its movement was transferred to the bāʾ, as it is said in you remained, remained, and as it is said of the liver, liver — the movement of the bāʾ was transferred to the kāf, when the bāʾ was stilled. And the people of the Arabic differed over the meaning of the what that is with evil is what: some of the grammarians of Basrah said: it is alone a noun, and that they disbelieve is its interpretation, as in, what an excellent man Zayd is; and that God send down is a substitute of He sent down. And some of the grammarians of Kūfah said: the meaning of that is, evil is the thing they purchased their souls with, that they disbelieve; and the what is the noun of evil, and that they disbelieve is the second noun.

And Kathīr reads the envy by its root: how evil is that for which they sold their own selves — the Jews sold the truth for falsehood, and hid the truth about Muḥammad; al-Suddī said: the Jews sold themselves — meaning, what is worse is what they chose for themselves, by disbelieving in what God revealed to Muḥammad ﷺ instead of believing, aiding and supporting him; and this behaviour of theirs is the result of their injustice and envy and hatred: grudging that God should reveal of His grace unto whom He wills of His servants — there is no envy worse than this. And of the double wrath, Ibn ʿAbbās commented: God became angry with them because they ignored some of the Torah, and disbelieved in the prophet that He sent to them; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: God became angry with them because of their disbelief in the Gospel and ʿĪsā, and He became angry with them again because they disbelieved in Muḥammad ﷺ and the Qurʾān; and the like was said by ʿIkrimah and Qatādah. The verse gives the envy its true name: it is not the envy of a grace given to another, but the refusal of the grace itself — and the wrath upon wrath is the measure of the refusal upon refusal.

---

## Verse 2:91

> When it is said to them: “Believe in what Allah has revealed,” they reply, “We only believe in what was sent down to us,” and they deny what came afterwards, though it is the truth confirming their own Scriptures! Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Why then did you kill Allah’s prophets before, if you are ˹truly˺ believers?”

**THE BELIEF THAT DIVIDES THE REVELATION**

The verse recounts the house's creed of the division. When it is said to them, believe in what Allah has sent down, they say, we believe in what was sent down to us — meaning, it is enough for us to believe in what was revealed to us in the Torah and the Gospel, and this is the path we choose — and they disbelieve in what came after it. The creed is the creed of the boundary: the revelation is accepted up to the boundary of the house and refused beyond it, as if the Lord stopped sending when the house stopped receiving. The verse answers the creed with the proof of the house itself: while it is the truth confirming what is with them — the Book they refuse is the Book that confirms their Book, and the confirming is the proof against them, since what was sent to Muhammad ﷺ conforms to what was revealed to the People of the Book, and their own Book is the witness for the one they disbelieve.

The verse then closes on the question that exposes the creed: say, why then did you kill the prophets of Allah before, if you are believers? The question returns the claim of the belief upon its makers: the ones who believe in what was sent down to them killed the prophets who brought it, and the killing of the prophets is the standing testimony against the claim of the belief. This is what al-Suddī said: in this verse Allah chastised the People of the Book for their saying we believe in what was sent down to us, while the killing of the prophets stands in their history. The killing is named among the curses of the house in the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān: (3:21 — **“Indeed, those who deny Allah’s signs, kill the prophets unjustly, and kill people who stand up for justice—give them good news of a painful punishment”**). The verse is the standing refutation of the religion that believes in the revelation of yesterday and disbelieves in the revelation of today: the belief that divides the revelation is not belief, and the killing of the prophets is the proof that the division is not from the Book but from the house.

**THE BEHIND AND THE MEANING OF THE BEHIND**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse clause by clause: and when it is said to them — when it is said to the Jews of the Children of Israel, who were between the two sides of the place of the emigration of the Messenger of God ﷺ: believe — that is, affirm — in what God has sent down — meaning, in what God sent down of the Qurʾān upon Muḥammad ﷺ; they say, we believe — that is, we affirm — in what was sent down upon us — meaning, the Torah that God sent down upon Mūsā. And He means by His saying, and they disbelieve in what is behind it, they deny, in what is behind it, meaning, in what is behind the Torah.

And the interpretation of behind it, in this place, is other than it; as it is said of the man who speaks with the good: there is nothing behind this speech — it is intended by it that there is not with the speaker of it anything other than that speech. So likewise the meaning of His saying, and they disbelieve in what is behind it, that is, in other than the Torah, and in what is after it of the Books of God, which He sent down to His messengers. And Qatādah said: and they disbelieve in what is behind it — he says, in what is after it; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, in the report of al-Rabīʿ: and they disbelieve in what is behind it, that is, in what is after it — meaning, after the Torah. And Kathīr gives the verse its argument: they say, we believe in what was sent down to us — meaning, it is enough for us to believe in what was revealed to us in the Torah and the Gospel, and this is the path that we choose; while it is the truth confirming what is with them — meaning, while knowing that what was revealed to Muḥammad ﷺ is the truth confirming what is with them; and this means that since what was sent to Muḥammad ﷺ conforms to what was revealed to the People of the Book, then this fact constitutes a proof against them. The division of the verse is read whole: the belief in what was sent down upon us, and the disbelief in what is behind it — and the Book is not divided, for the one who believes in its part and disbelieves in its behind has disbelieved in it all.

---

## Verse 2:92

> Indeed, Moses came to you with clear proofs, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully

**THE PROOFS AND THE CALF**

The verse gathers the house's history with the proofs into one sentence. Mūsā came to them with the clear proofs — the bayyināt, the signs and the evidences that he was the messenger of Allah and that there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah: the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, the staff and the shining hand, and the parting of the sea.
