Al-Baghawī reads the sending by its faces: whatever you send forth, for yourselves, of good — of the obedience and the righteous act, you will find it with God; and it was said: He intended, by the good, the wealth, as in His saying, (2:180 — **“if they leave something of value”**) — He intended, of the zakāh, or the charity; and you will find it with God — even the date, and the morsel, like Uḥud. Al-Alūsī reads the finding by its idiom: you will find it with God — meaning, you will find its reward with Him; and the speech is upon the omitting of the added; and it was said: the apparent is that the meaning is, you find it in the knowledge of God Most High, and His establishing of it in His knowledge is in the place of its establishing with Him. And al-Rabīʿ said of His saying, you will find it: he means, you will find its reward, with God. Al-Ṭabarī gives the whole verse its standing: We have made plain, in what passed, the meaning of the establishing of the prayer, and that it is its performing, with its limits and its obligations; and God means by His saying, and whatever you send forward, for yourselves, of good, you will find it with God: whatever act you do, of the righteous, in the days of your life, and send forward, before your death, as a store for yourselves, in your returning, you will find its reward with your Lord, on the Day of Resurrection, and He will recompense you by it.

Al-Qurṭubī sets over the sending its poetry: the poet said — send forth, for yourself, before your death, a righteous work, and act, for there is not, to the vacancy, a way; and another said — send forth, for yourself, a repentance hoped for, before the death, and before the binding of the tongues; and Abū l-ʿAtāhiyah said — be ready, with your wealth, in your life; and fairest of it all is the saying: be, for yourself, an heir, if you are able — for the one who makes himself his heir is rightly guided. The closing of the verse sets the seeing over the sending: al-Ṭabarī reads: this is a report from God to the believers He addressed with these verses, that whatever they do of good and of evil, secretly and openly, He is seeing of it, and nothing of it is hidden from Him, and He will recompense them, by the good-doing, the better, and by the evil-doing, its like; and the speech, though it came out the going out of the report, carries in it the promise and the threat, and the command and the restraining; and baṣīr is mubṣir, turned to baṣīr, as mubdiʿ was turned to badīʿ, and muʾlim to alīm. The sending is done in the days of the life, and the finding is done on the Day of the return — and between the two stands the seeing of the One who does not lose a date or a morsel.

---

## Verse 2:111

> The Jews and Christians each claim that none will enter Paradise except those of their own faith. These are their desires. Reply, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Show ˹me˺ your proof if what you say is true.”

**THE TWO CLAIMS IN ONE SENTENCE**

The two houses stand before the verse with two opposite claims, and the verse joins them into one saying. Al-Ṭabarī gives the joining its reason: it has been said that the meaning of that is other than what some go to — rather, the Jews said: none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew, and the Christians said: none will enter the Garden except the Christians; but since the meaning of the speech was understood by those addressed, the two parties were gathered in the one report, and it was said: and they said, none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian — meaning, the Jews said: none will enter the Garden except one who is Jewish, and the Christians said: none will enter the Garden except one who is Christian. Al-Baghawī carries the claim to its fullness: and that is that the Jews said: none will enter the Garden except one who is Jewish, and there is no religion but ours; and the Christians said the like. Al-Alūsī fixes the reference of the pronoun: and the pronoun is for the People of the Book, not for the many of them, as is suggested by the joining — and what is intended by them is the Jews and the Christians.

**THEIR WISHES, AND THE PROOF THEY ARE ASKED**

The answer of the verse is given in two words: the wishes, and the proof. Qatādah said of His saying, those are their wishes: wishes they wish upon God, false; and al-Rabīʿ said: wishes they wished upon God, without right. Al-Ṭabarī then carries the command: this is a command from God to His Prophet ﷺ to summon those who said, none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian, to a matter of justice between all the factions — their Muslims and their Jews and their Christians — which is the establishing of the proof against their claim; and God says to His Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ: O Muḥammad, say to the claimers that the Garden is entered by none but a Jew or a Christian, to the exclusion of the rest of mankind: bring your proof, of what you claim of that, and we will grant you your claim, if you are right in your claim. The proof is given its name by the ones asked for it: Qatādah said: bring your clear evidence; and al-Suddī said: bring your argument; and Mujāhid said: your argument; and al-Rabīʿ said the like. Al-Qurṭubī reads the word itself: the burhān is the proof that effects certainty, and its plural is barāhīn, like qurbān and qarābīn, and sulṭān and salāṭīn; and the original of hātū is hātiyū — the ḍammah was dropped for its heaviness, then the yāʾ was dropped for the meeting of the two still letters.

**THE REFUSAL THAT STANDS AS AN ANSWER**

Al-Ṭabarī closes the verse by its intent: this speech, though its apparent is the apparent of summoning the sayers of none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian to producing a proof for their claim, is in the meaning of God's declaring them false in their claim and their saying, because they were never able to produce a proof for that claim of theirs, ever; and His saying, nay, whoever submits his face to God, and he is a doer of good, makes plain that the speech is in the meaning of declaring false the Jews and the Christians, in their claim of what God reported of them. The sūrah of the Table gathers the standing of the claims, in their family: (5:18 — **“We are the children of Allah and His most beloved”**). The wish of the verse is measured by that claiming: it is the wish that closes the Garden upon the name, and the proof is asked of the one who closes — and the refusal of the proof is the answer of the verse.

---

## Verse 2:112

> But no! Whoever submits themselves to Allah and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**THE NAY THAT OPENS THE GARDEN**

The verse answers the two claims with the nay that opens them. Al-Ṭabarī reads: God means by His saying, nay — it is not as the claimers said, none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian; rather, whoever submits his face to God, and he is a doer of good, is the one who enters it and is delighted in it. Al-Suddī said: He informed them that the one who enters the Garden is the one who submits his face to God, the verse. Al-Ṭabarī gives the submitting its root in the tongue: by the submitting of the face, He means the humbling to His obedience and the yielding to His command; and the root of the islām is the surrendering, for it is from, I surrendered to his command, and that is the submitting to His command; and the Muslim is only named a Muslim by the submitting of his limbs to the obedience of his Lord. Al-Rabīʿ said of His saying, nay, whoever submits his face: he means, he made sincere for God. And the poet of the ḥanīfs, Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl, said before the sending: and I submitted my face to the One to whom the clouds submit, carrying sweet and cool water — meaning, I submitted to the obedience of the One to whose obedience the clouds submitted and were made compliant.

**THE FACE THAT STANDS FOR THE BODY**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the singling of the face its reason: God singled out, in the report of the one He reported of, the submitting of his face, rather than the rest of his limbs, because the noblest of the parts of the son of Ādam and his limbs is his face, and it is the greatest of them, upon him, in sanctity and right; so when his face, which is the noblest of the parts of his body, submits to a thing, the rest of the parts of his body are worthier of being more submissive to it; and for that reason the Arabs, in their speech, report of the thing and ascribe it to his face, meaning by that the very thing and its self; so the meaning of His saying, nay, whoever submits his face to God, is only: nay, whoever submits his body to God, and his body humbles to Him in obedience. Al-Saʿdī gathers the two conditions: the meaning is: whoever makes his outward and his inward sincerely for God, and follows the Messenger of God ﷺ, with the good-doing in his deed. And Ibn Kathīr states the law of the acceptance: for the deed to be accepted, there are two conditions — that the deed be done sincerely for God alone, and that it conform to the Law; and when the deed is sincere, but does not conform to the Law, it is not accepted; and this ḥadīth was recorded by Muslim — and so the good deeds of the monks and the rabbis are not accepted, even if they be sincerely for God alone, because they do not conform to the way of the Messenger ﷺ. The sūrah of the Women states the same submitting in the same wording: (4:125 — **“And who is better in faith than those who”**).

**THE REWARD AND THE TWO ABSENCES**

The reward of the verse is read by its nearness: his reward is with his Lord — the reward is not deferred to a hope and not exposed to a loss, but it is with the Lord, kept. Al-Ṭabarī reads the whole: nay, whoever makes sincere his obedience to God, and his worship of Him, doing good in that doing of his — his reward is with his Lord; and he said of the two absences: there is no fear upon them, and they do not grieve — that there is no fear upon them of the punishments of their Lord, on the Day of Resurrection, and they do not grieve for what they left behind them, of the world. The sūrah of Āl ʿImrān gives the community the same submission, in the face of the disputation: (3:20 — **“I have submitted myself to Allah, and so have my followers”**). The verse closes the claim of the two houses, then, not with a counter-claim but with a measure: the Garden is entered by the face that submits and the hand that does good, and the reward of that face is with the Lord of the Garden.

---

## Verse 2:113

> The Jews say, “The Christians have nothing to stand on” and the Christians say, “The Jews have nothing to stand on,” although both recite the Scriptures. And those ˹pagans˺ who have no knowledge say the same ˹about people of faith˺. Surely Allah will judge between them on the Day of Judgment regarding their dispute

**THE DISPUTATION AT THE PRESENCE OF THE PROPHET**

Ibn Isḥāq carries the verse its occasion, from Ibn ʿAbbās: when the people of Najrān, of the Christians, came upon the Messenger of God ﷺ, the scholars of the Jews came to them, and they disputed with one another before the Messenger of God ﷺ; and Rāfiʿ ibn Ḥuraymilah said: you are upon nothing — and he disbelieved in ʿĪsā the son of Maryam, and in the Gospel; and a man of the people of Najrān, of the Christians, said: you are upon nothing — and he denied the prophethood of Mūsā, and disbelieved in the Torah. And God sent down, upon that saying of the two of them: and the Jews say, the Christians are upon nothing, and the Christians say, the Jews are upon nothing, until His saying, in what they used to differ. Al-Baghawī names the two delegations: it was sent down concerning the Jews of the city, and the Christians of the people of Najrān; and when the delegation of Najrān came upon the Prophet ﷺ, the scholars of the Jews came to them, and they debated until their voices rose. Al-Rabīʿ said: these are the People of the Book who were in the days of the Prophet ﷺ.

**THE BOOK THAT EACH RECITES AGAINST THE OTHER**

Al-Ṭabarī raises the question the verse carries, and answers it: it was said — how can it be that the meaning is the denial, by each party of them, that the other party is upon anything, when each of the two parties was denying the prophethood of our Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ, at the time God sent down this verse? But the meaning of that is: the Jews said, the Christians are upon nothing of their religion, since they held to their religion, and the Christians said, the Jews are upon nothing, since they held to their religion. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the reading of the reciting: and they recite the Book — meaning, each recites in his Book the affirming of what he disbelieves in; the Jews disbelieve in ʿĪsā, while they have the Torah, in which is what God took upon them of the covenant, upon the tongue of Mūsā, for the affirming of ʿĪsā; and in the Gospel, of what ʿĪsā brought, is the affirming of Mūsā and what he brought, of the Torah, from God; and each disbelieves in what is in the hand of his companion. The reciting of the verse stands thus as the witness against the saying: the Book each house holds testifies against the nothing it declares of the other.

**THE ONES WHO KNOW NOT, AND THE JUDGMENT**

The third saying of the verse is given its people. Al-Qurṭubī carries the two readings: what is intended by those who know not, in the saying of the majority, is the disbelievers of the Arabs, because they have no Book; and ʿAṭāʾ said: what is intended are nations that were before the Jews and the Christians; and al-Suddī said the like. Al-Ṭabarī reads the likeness: likewise said those who know not, the like of their saying — the nations that passed before the Jews and the Christians, and before the Torah and the Gospel, said the like of what these two parties said, each to its companion; and God gathered the three sayings in one standing — the saying of the house upon the house, and the saying of the ones who have no Book upon the people of the Book. And the verse closes with the judgment: God will judge between them, on the Day of Resurrection, in what they used to differ — each party of them claims that its companion is upon nothing, and each recites a Book that testifies against its claim; and the judgment of the Day is the answer the disputation did not receive in the world — (22:17 — **“Allah will judge between them ˹all˺ on Judgment Day”**).

---

## Verse 2:114

> Who does more wrong than those who prevent Allah’s Name from being mentioned in His places of worship and strive to destroy them? Such people have no right to enter these places except with fear. For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**THE ONES WHO STROVE IN ITS RUIN**

The verse names the wrong of the masjid, and the reports divide the ones who did it between two houses and one city. Al-Baghawī carries the first occasion: it was sent down concerning Ṭīṭūs the son of Isbīsyānūs the Roman, and his companions — they raided the Children of Israel, and killed their fighters, and took captive their children, and burned the Torah, and ruined Bayt al-Maqdis, and cast the carrion into it, and slaughtered the swine in it; and it remained a ruin. And Qatādah and al-Suddī said: it is Bukhtnaṣṣar and his companions, who raided the Jews, and ruined Bayt al-Maqdis, and the Christians aided them in that — Ṭīṭūs the Roman and his companions, of the people of Rūm; al-Suddī said: because they killed Yaḥyā the son of Zakariyyā; and Qatādah said: some of the Jews carried them to the aiding of Bukhtnaṣṣar the Babylonian. The ruin of the verse is thus done twice, by two empires, upon one House, and the aiding of one house of the Book in the ruin of the other's House stands in the reports as its measure.

**THE ONES WHO TURNED HIM FROM THE HOUSE**

Others carried the verse to the Ḥaram. Ibn ʿAbbās said: they are the Christians; and Mujāhid said: the Christians used to cast the filth into Bayt al-Maqdis, and prevent the people from praying in it. And others said: rather, God intended, by this verse, the polytheists of Quraysh, when they prevented the Messenger of God ﷺ from the Inviolable Masjid. Ibn Zayd carries that scene: these are the polytheists, when they came between the Messenger of God ﷺ, on the day of al-Ḥudaybiyyah, and his entering Makkah, until he slaughtered his offering at Dhī Ṭuwā, and made a truce with them; and he said to them: no one was ever turned away from this House, and a man used to meet the killer of his father or his brother in it, and he would not repel him; and they said: we will not be entered upon by the ones who killed our fathers at Badr, while we remain! And Ibn Kathīr carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: Quraysh prevented the Prophet ﷺ from praying at the Kaʿbah, in al-Masjid al-Ḥarām, so God revealed the verse.

Al-Ṭabarī decides between the sayings by the ruin: the proof of the soundness of what we said is that the argument has established that there is no saying, in the meaning of this verse, but one of the three sayings we mentioned, and that there is no masjid God intended by His saying, and strives in its ruin, but one of the two masjids — either the masjid of Bayt al-Maqdis, or the Inviolable Masjid; and since it is known that the polytheists of Quraysh never strove in the ruining of the Inviolable Masjid, though they prevented the Messenger of God ﷺ and his Companions from praying in it, at some times — it is established that the ones God described as striving in the ruin of His masjids are other than the ones He described as maintaining them; since the polytheists of Quraysh built the Inviolable Masjid in the jāhiliyyah, and by its maintenance was their boasting. The wrong of the verse is thus read by its two deeds: the preventing of the Name from being mentioned, which the Quraysh did, and the striving in the ruin, which the empires did — and the question of who is more wrong stands over the two.

**THE ENTERING IN FEAR, AND THE TWO PUNISHMENTS**

The verse then gives the ones who strove their entry. Ibn ʿAbbās said of His saying, those ones — it was not for them to enter it, except fearing: no Roman entered Bayt al-Maqdis, after its building, except fearing — if it had been known of him, he would have been killed; and Qatādah and Muqātil said: no Christian enters Bayt al-Maqdis except fearing. Ibn Zayd carries the Ḥaram's fulfillment: the Messenger of God ﷺ called out: no polytheist performs the ḥajj after this year, and none circumambulates the House naked; and the polytheists used to say: O God, we have been prevented from descending! And al-Suddī gives the two punishments their faces: as for their disgrace in this world — when al-Mahdī rises and al-Qusṭanṭīniyyah is opened, he will kill them, and that is the disgrace; and as for the great punishment, it is the punishment of Jahannam, which is not lightened for its people, and they are not killed in it, that they die. Ibn Kathīr gathers the fulfillment of the House: this verse carries the good news from God to the Muslims, that He will give them possession of al-Masjid al-Ḥarām and all the masjids, and disgrace the idolaters; and no idolater enters the House after that, except in fear of being seized or killed, unless he embraces Islam; and God fulfilled that promise. The sūrah of the Ḥajj states the law of the repelling over all the houses of the Name: (22:40 — **“destruction would have surely claimed monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques”**).

---

## Verse 2:115

> To Allah belong the east and the west, so wherever you turn you are facing ˹towards˺ Allah. Surely Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing

**THE OWNERSHIP OF THE TWO HORIZONS**

The verse opens with the ownership of the directions, before it speaks of the turning. Al-Qurṭubī reads: the east is the place of the rising, and the west is the place of the setting — the two are His, a possession, and what is between them of the directions and the creatures, by the bringing into being and the creating; and He singled out the two, with the mention and the ascription to Himself, for honoring, like the House of God, and the she-camel of God. Al-Alūsī reads the owning by its reach: He means the two known directions, that neighbor the point from which the sun rises and sets; and He alludes, by His owning of the two, to the owning of all the earth; and some said: when the earth is spherical, every east is a west by relation, and all the earth is so. The owning of the verse is the ground of the turning: the direction the worshipper faces is owned, in all of its faces, by the One he turns to.

**THE OCCASIONS OF THE TURNING**

The reports give the verse its occasions, and they are four. Ibn ʿAbbās said: a group of the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ went out on a journey, before the turning of the qiblah to the Kaʿbah, and a mist befell them, and the prayer came, and they sought the qiblah by judgment and prayed; and when the mist lifted, it became clear to them that they had prayed to other than the qiblah; and they asked, and the verse was sent down. ʿĀmir ibn Rabīʿah carries the night scene: we were with the Messenger of God ﷺ, on a dark and black night, and we alighted at a place, and a man used to take stones and make a masjid, and pray in it; and when we reached the morning, we found we had prayed to other than the qiblah; and we said: O Messenger of God, we have prayed our night to other than the qiblah! And God sent down: and to God belong the east and the west, to the end of the verse. And Ibn ʿUmar said: this verse — wherever you turn, there is the Face of God — was sent down only for you to pray wherever your mount faces, on a journey, voluntarily; the Messenger of God ﷺ, when he returned from Makkah, used to pray voluntary prayer upon his mount, gesturing with his head, toward Madīnah. Qatādah carries the standing before the abrogation: they used to pray toward Bayt al-Maqdis, while the Messenger of God ﷺ was in Makkah, before the emigration, and after he emigrated, the Messenger of God ﷺ prayed toward Bayt al-Maqdis sixteen months, then he was turned after that toward the Kaʿbah, the Inviolable House; and God abrogated it by another verse (2:144 — **“So turn your face towards the Sacred Mosque”**).

**THE FACE, AND THE ABROGATION**

The verse was read, upon these reports, by two faces. Some said: God sent down this verse before He made obligatory, upon His Prophet ﷺ and the believers, the turning toward the Inviolable Masjid — He sent it down only to teach His Prophet and his Companions that they may turn their faces, in the prayer, toward whatever they wish of the sides of the east and the west, for they do not turn their faces to any side of it but that God is there, in His knowledge and His encompassing; and the abrogation of the qiblah is not from this verse, for the abrogating thing is only by an abrogated thing, and no argument has been established, that must be submitted to, that this verse was sent down for the turning toward the Kaʿbah. Others held the saying of Ibn ʿAbbās: the verse is abrogated by His saying, and wherever you are, turn your faces toward its direction — as if he permitted, in the beginning, that a man pray however he wished, then that was abrogated; and Qatādah said: the abrogating is His saying, so turn your face toward the Inviolable Masjid. The ijmaʿ stands over the two readings: they agreed that it is not permissible for a sound man to pray an obligatory prayer to other than the qiblah. The verse keeps, then, its ownership over the abrogation: the east and the west are His in the permitting and in the turning alike — and the All-Encompassing, at the close of the verse, is the name that holds the directions, before the turning and after it.

---

## Verse 2:116

> They say, “Allah has offspring.” Glory be to Him! In fact, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth—all are subject to His Will

**THE SAYING, AND THE GLORYING THAT ANSWERS IT**

The verse returns to the house's saying of the Garden, and gives it its root: the claiming of the nearness. God reports their saying, and then answers it with the glorying that stands before every answer — glory be Him! Then He brings the ownership that refutes it: nay, to Him belongs what is in the heavens and the earth. Al-Ṭabarī reads the refutation by its order: God declared false those who claimed that God has a child, by His saying, nay, to Him belongs what is in the heavens and the earth, a possession and a creation; then He reported, of all that is in the heavens and the earth, that they are confessing, by their evidence, to their Lord and their Creator, and that God is the One who brought them into being and made them — though some of them deny that, their tongues are submissive to Him in obedience, by their testifying to Him, with the effects of the making that are in them; and the Messiah is one of them — how then can God have a child, when this is his attribute? And this is a report from God that the Messiah — whom the Christians claimed to be the son of God — declares them false, he, and the heavens and the earth and what is in them, either by the tongue or by the evidence; for God reported, of all of them, their obedience to Him and their confessing to Him in servitude, immediately after His saying, and they said, God has taken a child.

**THE QĀNITŪN, BY THE FACES OF THE WORD**

The qunūt of the verse is read by its meanings in the tongue. Al-Ṭabarī gathers: the qunūt, in the speech of the Arabs, has meanings — one of them is the obedience, and another is the standing, and the third is the refraining from speech and the withholding from it. Qatādah said, of His saying, all of them are qānitūn to Him: obedient; and Mujāhid said: obedient — he said: the obedience of the disbeliever is in the prostration of his shadow, while he is averse; and in another report: by the prostration of his shadow, while he is averse. Al-Suddī said: all are obedient to Him, on the Day of Resurrection; and ʿIkrimah said: the obedience. Ibn ʿAbbās said: obedient. And others said: the meaning is, all are confessing to Him in servitude — ʿIkrimah said: all are confessing to Him in servitude. And al-Rabīʿ said: all are standing, for Him, on the Day of Resurrection.

Al-Ṭabarī decides the worthiest of the meanings: the worthiest of the meanings of the qunūt, in His saying, all of them are qānitūn to Him, is the obedience and the confessing, to God, in servitude, by the testifying of their bodies, with what is in them of the effects of the making, and the evidence of the oneness of God — that God is the One who brought them into being and created them. And some of those whose knowledge fell short of directing the speech to its direction claimed that His saying, all of them are qānitūn to Him, is particular to the people of the obedience, and not general; and it is not permissible to claim a particularity in a verse whose apparent is general, except by an argument that must be submitted to. The sūrah of Maryam states the same saying, of another house of the claim: (19:88 — **“The Most Compassionate has offspring”**). The glorying of the verse stands over the sayings of the two houses alike — and the qunūt of all that is in the heavens and the earth, even of the one who claims the offspring for Him, is the witness the verse brings against the claim.

---

## Verse 2:117

> ˹He is˺ the Originator of the heavens and the earth! When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, “Be!” And it is

**THE ORIGINATOR WITHOUT A MODEL**

The verse gives the glorying of the verse before its Maker. Al-Qurṭubī reads the name: badīʿ is of the form faʿīl, for the intensifying, and it is raised as the predicate of a suppressed subject; and the active participle is mubdiʿ, like baṣīr from mubṣir; one says, I originated the thing — without a model; and God Most High is the badīʿ of the heavens and the earth — meaning, the One who brought them into being and established them and originated them and invented them, upon other than a limit and a model; and al-Rabīʿ said of His saying, the badīʿ of the heavens and the earth: He originated their creation, and no one shared Him in their creation. The name stands, in the verse, immediately after the refuting of the offspring: the One who originates without a model has no model from which a child could be born.

**THE DECREED MATTER AND THE WORD**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the word of the decree its root in the tongue: the root of every decree of a matter is the perfecting of it and the finishing from it; and from it, the one who judges between the people is called the qāḍī between them, for his severing of the judgment between the litigants and his cutting of the ruling between them and his finishing from it; and from it, it is said of the dead man, he has decreed — meaning, he has finished from the world and been severed from it; and from it, it is said, my wonder at so-and-so does not expire — meaning, it is not cut off; and from it is the saying of God, and your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him — meaning, He severed the ruling in it, between His servants, by His commanding them with that. He then raises the question the questioners raise, and answers it: if it is said to us — what is the meaning of His saying, and when He decrees a matter, He only says to it, be, and it is, and in what state does He say, to the matter He decrees, be? Is it in the state of its nonexistence, and that is a state in which His commanding it is not permissible, since it is impossible to command except the one commanded of, so that when there is no one commanded of, the commanding is impossible? Or does He say that to it in the state of its existence, and that is a state in which His commanding it with coming into being is not permissible, because it is a coming-into-being that exists, and it is not said to the existing one, be existing, except without the meaning of the commanding of his very self? And the worthiest of the sayings, with correctness, is that it be said: it is general, in all that God decreed and brought into being, because its apparent is the apparent of generality; and God's commanding of a thing, when He wills to bring it into being, with be, is in the state of His willing it to be brought into being — the existence of the thing He willed to bring into being does not precede His willing of it, nor His commanding of it with being and existence, and it does not lag behind it; and a thing is not commanded of with existence, and willed so, except that it is existing, and it is not existing except that it is commanded of with existence, and willed so.

**THE READING AND THE BLINK**

The grammarians read the standing of the word. Some of them permitted the accusative of yakūn, of the one who read, Our saying to a thing, when We will it, is only that We say to it, be, and it is — by the meaning: that We say, and it be; and the reading of the raising is upon the report — and it is. Al-Qurṭubī gives the second matter of the verse its place in the science: every innovation that proceeds from a created one does not but have, in the Law, an origin, or not; and if it has an origin, it falls under the generality of what God called to, and His Messenger singled out — it is in the domain of the praise; and if its model is not existing, as with a kind of the giving and the generosity and the doing of the good — this is of the praiseworthy innovation. The verse closes with the single word that needs no repetition: He only says to it, be, and it is; and the sūrah of the Moon gives the word its speed: (54:50 — **“done in the blink of an eye”**), and the sūrah of Maryam gives the decree its form: (19:35 — **“It is not for Allah to take a son! Glory be to Him”**). The offspring of the claim is answered by the Originator who decrees — and the be that needs neither a model nor a mother.

---

## Verse 2:118

> Those who have no knowledge say, “If only Allah would speak to us or a sign would come to us!” The same was said by those who came before. Their hearts are all alike. Indeed, We have made the signs clear for people of sure faith

**THE ASKING OF THE ONE WHO KNOWS NOT**

Ibn Isḥāq carries the verse its occasion, from Ibn ʿAbbās: Rāfiʿ ibn Ḥuraymilah said to the Messenger of God ﷺ: if you are a messenger from God, as you say, then say to God, and let Him speak to us, until we hear His speech! And God sent down, upon that of his saying: and those who know not say, if only God would speak to us, or a sign come to us — the verse, all of it. The ones who know not are given their faces in the reports: Mujāhid said: the Christians say it; and al-Rabīʿ said: they are the disbelievers of the Arabs; and al-Suddī and Qatādah said: the polytheists of the Arabs. Al-Qurṭubī gathers the sayings: Ibn ʿAbbās said: they are the Jews; Mujāhid: the Christians — and al-Ṭabarī preferred it, because they are the ones mentioned in the verse first; and al-Rabīʿ and al-Suddī and Qatādah: the polytheists of the Arabs. The lūlā of the verse is given its tongue: it is in the meaning of hallā, an urging — and it is not the lūlā that gives the preventing of a thing, for the existence of another; and the difference between the two, with the people of the tongue, is that the lūlā in the meaning of the urging is not followed except by a verb, expressed or estimated, and the one of the preventing is followed by a subject. The asking is thus read by its form: it is the urging of the one who knows not, upon the One who knows.

**THE SAYING OF THE ONES BEFORE**

The verse then joins the askers of the city to the askers before them. Mujāhid said of His saying, likewise said those before them, the like of their saying: they are the Jews. Al-Rabīʿ said: He means the Jews and the Christians. Al-Ṭabarī decides: God showed that those He intended, by His saying, and those who know not say, if only God would speak to us, are the Christians, and the ones who said the like of their saying are the Jews — who asked Mūsā to show them their Lord openly, and to let them hear the speech of their Lord, and asked, of the signs, what it is not theirs to ask, in their ruling over their Lord; and likewise, the Christians wished upon their Lord, in their ruling over Him, that He let them hear His speech, and show them what they willed, of the signs; and God reported of them that they said, of the saying in that, the like of what the Jews said, and wished upon their Lord the like of their wishes; and their saying is only like the saying of the Jews, because of the likeness of their hearts, in the misguidance and the disbelief in God; and Mujāhid said of His saying, their hearts are alike: the hearts of the Christians and the Jews; and al-Rabīʿ said: He means the Arabs, and the Jews, and the Christians, and other than them. The likeness of the verse is thus a likeness of the hearts, not of the tongues: the asking of the city is the same asking of the mountain, because the heart that asks is the same heart.

**THE SIGNS THAT ARE ALREADY CLEAR**

The verse answers the asking of the sign with the signs already made clear: We have made the signs clear — for a people who are certain. The ones who ask for a sign to come are answered by the signs that have come, and the certainty of the verse is the condition of their seeing. The sūrah of the Women states the asking of the house, upon the mountain, in the same wording: (4:153 — **“They demanded what is even greater than this from Moses”**). The asking of the verse is measured, then, by the asking before it: the thunderbolt answered the ones who asked to see, and the clear signs answer the ones who ask for a sign — and the likeness of the hearts is the reason the asking is repeated, from house to house, until it reached the city.

---

## Verse 2:119

> We have surely sent you with the truth ˹O Prophet˺ as a deliverer of good news and a warner. And you will not be accountable for the residents of the Hellfire

**THE SENDING AND ITS TWO FACES**

The verse closes the passage of the asking with the standing of the Messenger between the sending and the account. Al-Qurṭubī reads the two faces: a bearer of good news is installed as a state, and a warner is joined upon it; and their meaning has preceded. Al-Ṭabarī reads the whole: God narrated the tales of peoples, of the Jews and the Christians, and mentioned their misguidance, and their disbelief in God, and their boldness upon His prophets; then He said to His Prophet ﷺ: We have sent you, O Muḥammad, with the truth, a bearer of good news — to whoever believes in you and follows you, of those whose news I have narrated to you, and those whose news I have not narrated to you; and a warner — to whoever disbelieves in you and opposes you; so convey My message; and upon you is nothing, of the deeds of whoever disbelieves in you, after your conveying to him My message, of a following, and you are not asked of what he did after that.

**THE REPORT OF THE TWO PARENTS, AND ITS REFUSAL**

Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb al-Quraẓī carries the verse an occasion, and al-Ṭabarī refuses it: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: would that I knew what my two parents did! And it was sent down: and you are not asked of the companions of the Blaze; and in another report: he said it three times, and it was sent down: We have sent you with the truth, a bearer of good news and a warner, and you are not asked of the companions of the Blaze — and he did not mention them, two, until God took him. Al-Ṭabarī states his position: the correctness, with me, of the reading of that, is the reading of the one who read it with the raising, upon the report — because God narrated the tales of peoples, then said to His Prophet ﷺ: We have sent you, O Muḥammad, with the truth, a bearer of good news and a warner; so convey the message; and upon you is no following of the deeds of whoever disbelieves in you, and you are not asked of what they did; and no mention of the asking of the Messenger of God ﷺ of his Lord, of the companions of the Blaze, has run, for His saying, and you are not asked of the companions of the Blaze, to have a face it is directed to. And if one supposes the report of Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb to be sound, then in the impossibility of the doubting, of the Messenger, that the people of the association are of the people of the Blaze, and that his two parents were of them, is what repels the correctness of what he said; and God's beginning of the report, after His saying, We have sent you with the truth, a bearer of good news and a warner, with the wāw — by His saying, and you are not asked of the companions of the Blaze — and His leaving of the joining of that to its first, with the fāʾ, that it would be, We have sent you with the truth, a bearer of good news and a warner, so you are not asked of the companions of the Blaze — is the clearest evidence that the report, by His saying, and you are not asked, is severed from its first. And the reading of Ibn Masʿūd and Ubayy repels the reading of the state, because their inserting of what they inserted, of mā and lan, indicates the severing of the speech from its first, and the beginning of His saying, and you are not asked; and when it is a beginning, it is not a state. Muqātil carries another occasion: the Prophet ﷺ said: if only God sent down His might upon the Jews, they would believe! And God sent down: and you are not asked of the companions of the Blaze.

**THE CONVEYING AND THE RECKONING**

Ibn Kathīr reads the negation by its reach: We shall not ask you of the disbelief of the ones who rejected you; and he brings the family of the verse: (13:40 — **“your duty is only to deliver”**) and (88:21 — **“˹continue to˺ remind ˹all, O Prophet˺, for your duty is only to remind”**). The verse stands, then, as the boundary of the Messenger's labor: the sending is with the truth, and the two faces are the good news and the warning, and the account of the Blaze is with its Lord — the Messenger conveys, and the Blaze receives, and between the two stands the message that has been delivered.

---

## Verse 2:120

> Never will the Jews or Christians be pleased with you, until you follow their faith. Say, “Allah’s guidance is the only ˹true˺ guidance.” And if you were to follow their desires after ˹all˺ the knowledge that has come to you, there would be none to protect or help you against Allah

**THE PLEASURE THAT IS NOT REACHABLE**

The verse closes the passage of the two houses with the summing of their pleasure. Al-Baghawī carries the occasion: that is that they used to ask the Prophet ﷺ the truce, and make him hope that, if he granted them respite, they would follow him; and God sent down this verse — its meaning is, if you make truce with them, they are not pleased with it, and they only ask you the truce to deceive. Al-Qurṭubī reads the asking by its intent: their intent, O Muḥammad, by what they propose of the signs, is not to believe; rather, if you brought them all they ask, they would not be pleased with you. Al-Ṭabarī gives the negation its reason: the Jews and the Christians are never pleased with you, ever — so leave the seeking of what pleases them and agrees with them, and turn to the seeking of the pleasure of God, in your calling them to what God sent you with, of the truth; for what you call them to, of that, is the way to the gathering, with you, upon the affection and the upright religion; and you have no way to their pleasing, by the following of their faith — because the Judaism is the opposite of the Christianity, and the Christianity is the opposite of the Judaism, and the Christianity and the Judaism do not gather in one person, in one state; and the Jews and the Christians do not gather upon the pleasure with you, except that you be a Jewish Christian, and that is of what will never be of you — for you are one person, and two opposite religions do not gather in you, in one state. The negation of the verse is thus a mercy as much as a report: the pleasure that is asked of is the one that cannot be, and the Messenger is turned from its seeking to the seeking of the pleasure that can be.

**THE GUIDANCE, AND THE DESIRES**

The verse gives the answer its word: say, the guidance of God is the guidance. Al-Ṭabarī reads: God said to His Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ: say, O Muḥammad, to these Christians and Jews who said, none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian — the guidance of God is the guidance: the statement of God is the satisfying statement, and the severing judgment, between us; so come to the Book of God, and His statement, in which He made plain, for His servants, what they differed in — and it is the Torah, which you all affirm is from God; it will become clear to you, in it, who of us is the one of the truth, and who is the one of the falsehood, and which of us is the people of the Garden, and which is the people of the Fire. And God commanded His Prophet to call them to the guidance of God and His statement, because in it is the declaring false of the Jews and the Christians, in their saying that the Garden is entered by none but a Jew or a Christian, and the making plain of the affair of Muḥammad, and that the one who denies him is of the people of the Fire, without the one who affirms him. It was said: God sent down this verse upon His Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ because the Jews and the Christians called him to their religions, and each party of them said: the guidance is what we are upon, without what is upon it, of the other than us, of the rest of the religions; and God admonished him against doing that, and taught him the severing argument, between them, in what each party of them claimed.

**THE CONDITION THAT CLOSES THE PASSAGE**

The verse then closes with the condition against the Messenger — and the condition is the passage's final guard. Al-Ṭabarī reads: God means, by His saying, and if you follow — O Muḥammad — the desire of these Jews and Christians, in what pleases them of you, of the becoming Jewish and the becoming Christian, and you arrive, by that, at their pleasing, and agree, in it, with their love — after the knowledge that came to you, of their misguidance and their disbelief in their Lord, and after what I narrated to you, of their news, in this sūrah — you have not, from God, a guardian — meaning, you have not, O Muḥammad, a guardian who tends your affair, and a sustainer who stands with it; and no helper, who helps you against God, and repels from you what descends upon you of His punishment, and prevents you from that, if your Lord makes it descend upon you. Al-Qurṭubī reads the address by its reach: in this address are two faces — one of them, that it is for the Messenger, by the directing of the address to him; and the second, that it is for the Messenger, and what is intended by it is his community. And he reads the desires by their number: the desires is the plural of the desire, as you say, a camel and camels; and when they were different, they were pluralized; and if it had been carried upon the singular of the faith, he would have said, their desire. The passage of the two houses closes, then, upon three standings: the pleasure that is not reached, the guidance that is the guidance, and the condition that guards the Messenger — and after him, his community — from the following of the desires, after the knowledge has come — and the Book states the summing of the ways, in the sūrah of Āl ʿImrān: (3:85 — **“Whoever seeks a way other than Islam”**).

**THE ONE MILLAH OF THE VERSE**

Al-Qurṭubī gives the faith of the verse its place in the ruling: a group of the scholars held to this verse — among them Abū Ḥanīfah, and al-Shāfiʿī, and Dāwūd, and Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal — upon that the disbelief is all of it one faith, by His saying, their faith, and He made the faith one, and by His saying, You have your path, and I have my Path, and by his ﷺ saying: the people of the two faiths do not inherit from one another — upon that what is intended by it is the Islam and the disbelief, by the evidence of his ﷺ saying: the Muslim does not inherit the disbeliever. And Mālik, and Aḥmad in the other report, went to that the disbelief is faiths, and the Jew does not inherit the Christian, and the two do not inherit the Magian, taking the apparent of his ﷺ saying: the people of the two faiths do not inherit from one another; and as for His saying, their faith, what is intended by it is the many, though it is one in the wording, by the evidence of its ascription to the pronoun of the many. The faith of the verse is thus read by its singling: it is one, of the two houses, in the wording of the verse — and the ruling of the inheritance stands upon that singling, between the ones who differ in it.

---

## Verse 2:121

> Those We have given the Book follow it as it should be followed. It is they who ˹truly˺ believe in it. As for those who reject it, it is they who are the losers

**THE ONES WHO WERE GIVEN THE BOOK**

The verse turns from the two houses in their claims to the ones among them who received the Book as it was meant to be received. The people of the verse are read by two faces. Qatādah said of His saying, those We gave them the Book: these are the Companions of the prophet of God ﷺ — they believed in the Book of God and affirmed it. And others said: rather, God intended, by that, the scholars of the Children of Israel, who believed in God and affirmed His messengers, and acknowledged the ruling of the Torah, and worked by what God commanded in it, of the following of Muḥammad ﷺ, and the belief in him, and the affirming of what he brought from God. Al-Ṭabarī decides between the two: this saying is worthier of correctness than the saying of Qatādah — because the verses before it passed with the reports of the People of the two Books, and the changing, of the one who changed of them, the Book of God, and their interpreting of it upon other than its interpretation, and their claiming upon God the falsehoods; and no mention has run, of the Companions of Muḥammad ﷺ, in the verse before it, for the saying to be directed to the report of them, nor of them is there mention in the verse that follows it. Al-Qurṭubī carries the third face: Ibn Zayd said: they are the ones who embraced Islam, of the Children of Israel; and the Book, upon this interpretation, is the Torah; and the verse is general. Al-Saʿdī gathers the standing of the people of the verse: God reports that those He gave the Book, and bestowed it upon them, in an absolute bestowing — they follow it with the truth of its following; and the following is the tilāwah — they make lawful its lawful, and make unlawful its unlawful, and work by its muḥkam, and believe in its mutashābih; and these are the fortunate ones of the People of the Book, who knew the blessing of God and thanked it, and believed in all the messengers, and did not differentiate between any of them.

**THE TRUE RECITATION**

The ḥaqq of the recitation is given its definition by the ones who carried it. Ibn ʿAbbās said of His saying, they recite it with the truth of its recitation: they make lawful its lawful, and make unlawful its unlawful, and they do not distort it. And ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said, by the One in whose hand is my soul: the truth of its recitation is to make lawful its lawful, and make unlawful its unlawful, and to recite it as God sent it down, and not to displace the words from their places, and not to interpret anything of it upon other than its interpretation. Qatādah said: they made lawful its lawful, and made unlawful its unlawful, and worked by what is in it; and it was reported to us that Ibn Masʿūd used to say: the truth of its recitation is to make lawful its lawful, and make unlawful its unlawful, and to recite it as God sent it down, and not to displace it from its places. Al-Saʿdī reads the word itself: and the tilāwah is the following. Al-Qurṭubī carries the report upon it: Naṣr ibn ʿĪsā reported, from Mālik, from Nāfiʿ, from Ibn ʿUmar, from the Prophet ﷺ, of His saying, they recite it with the truth of its recitation, that he said: they follow it with the truth of its following — in its chain is more than one unknown narrator, as al-Khaṭīb Abū Bakr mentioned, but its meaning is sound. The recitation of the verse is thus the following of the Book — and the ḥaqq of it is the standing at its lawful and its unlawful, before the moving of the tongue. The Book states the same standing, of the ones who were given the Book before this Qurʾān: (28:52 — **“those ˹faithful˺ to whom We had given the Scripture before this ˹Quran˺, they do believe in it”**).

**THE LOSERS ON THE OTHER SIDE**

The verse closes with the other side of the Book: and whoever disbelieves in it — those are the losers. The ones who recite with the truth of the recitation are the believers in it, and the believers in it are the ones who follow it — and the one who rejects it, of the ones who were given it, is the loser; for the Book that is not followed becomes the witness against its carrier, and the loss of the verse is the loss of the one who carried the Book and left its ḥaqq behind.

---

## Verse 2:122

> O Children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you and how I honoured you above the others

**THE RETURN OF THE ADDRESS**

The sūrah returns to the house of Israel, after the passage of Ibrāhīm opens, and it returns with the same address that opened its rebuke at the beginning of the sūrah. Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse by its place: this verse is an admonition from God to the Jews who were between the two sides of the emigration of the Messenger of God ﷺ, and a reminding, of Him to them, of what passed of His hands upon them, in His doing with their forefathers, to draw them, by it, to His religion and the affirming of His Messenger Muḥammad ﷺ; so He said: O Children of Israel, remember My hands upon you, and My doings with you, and My rescuing of you from the hand of your enemy Firʿawn and his people, and My sending down upon you the mann and the salwā, in your wandering, and My establishing you in the lands, after you were humbled and overpowered, and My singling out the messengers from among you, and My preferring you over the world of the ones among whom you lived, so long as you were in My obedience — by the following of My messenger to you, and the affirming of him, and the affirming of what he brought you from Me; and leave the persisting in the misguidance and the error. And He said: We have mentioned, in what passed, the blessings God bestowed upon the Children of Israel, and the meanings with which He reminded them of His bounties, and the world they were preferred over — by the reports and the witnesses, and We disliked lengthening the book by its repetition, since the meaning, in this place and there, is one.

**THE REPEAT, AND ITS REASON**

Al-Alūsī gives the repeat its reason: it is a repetition, for the reminding of the Children of Israel, and a re-doing of their warning, for the exaggeration of the sincere counsel, and for the announcing that that is the summing of the story and its intent. Ibn Kathīr gives the repeat its occasion: the like of this verse was mentioned at the beginning of the sūrah, and it is mentioned here to emphasize the importance of the following of the unlettered Prophet and Messenger, who is described, for the People of the Scriptures, in their Books, by his traits, and his name, and the good news of him, and the description of his community; and God warned them against concealing that — and it is of the blessings God granted them; and they should not envy their cousins, the Arabs, for what God gave them, of the Final Messenger of God being an Arab — envy should not incite them to opposing the Prophet, or denying him, or refraining from following him. The verse is thus the same summons as the first — and its return, after the passage of the two houses and the passage of the Messenger's standing with them, is the sign that the summons is still open: the remembering of the blessing is asked of them again, at the door of the Ibrāhīm passage, because the blessing they are to remember is now the blessing of the prophet of their own description, and the envied one is their cousin. (2:47 — **“O Children of Israel! Remember ˹all˺ the favours I granted you and how I honoured you above the others”**).

---

## Verse 2:123

> And guard yourselves against the Day when no soul will be of any help to another. No ransom will be taken, no intercession accepted, and no help will be given

**THE DAY THAT RETURNS**

The verse returns, with the address, its Day. Al-Ṭabarī reads it by its standing: this verse is a frightening, from God, of the ones His admonition preceded, in the verse before it; and God says to them: fear — O assembly of the Children of Israel, who change My Book and My sending down, who displace its interpretation from its face, who call My Messenger Muḥammad ﷺ a liar — the punishment of a Day in which no soul settles, for another soul, anything, and it does not avail it any availing; and fear that you perish upon what you are upon, of your disbelief in Me, and your calling My Messenger a liar, and die upon it; for it is a Day in which no ransom is accepted from a soul, for what has befallen it, and no intercessor intercedes for what has become obligatory upon it of a right, and no helper helps it against God, when He takes vengeance upon it, by its disobedience of Him. And he said: the explanation of all the meanings of this verse has passed, in its like before, and that dispenses with its repetition in this place.

**THE FOUR ABSENCES**

Al-Alūsī reads the variation of the repeat: the expression varies — the intercession came first with the wording of the acceptance, preceding the ransom, and here with the wording of the benefit, delayed after it; and it is, as was said, an indication of the negating of the very root of the thing, and the negating of what is built upon it; and what preceded was for the command of the standing with the rights of the preceding blessings, and what is here is for the reminding of a blessing by which He preferred them over the worlds — and it is the blessing of the belief in the prophet of their time, and their yielding to his rulings, that they may seize it and believe and be of the ones preferred, not the ones preferring. The four absences of the verse stand thus over the house that counted upon its names: no soul avails another, and no ransom is taken, and no intercession benefits, and no help is given — and the house that said, none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian, is answered, again, by the Day in which the names do not pay. (2:48 — **“Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another”**).

---

## Verse 2:124

> ˹Remember˺ when Abraham was tested by his Lord with ˹certain˺ commandments, which he fulfilled. Allah said, “I will certainly make you into a role model for the people.” Abraham asked, “What about my offspring?” Allah replied, “My covenant is not extended to the wrongdoers.”

**THE PASSAGE OF IBRĀHĪM OPENS**

The sūrah turns from the house of Israel to the father of the house, and the turn is given its reason by the mention that precedes it. Al-Qurṭubī reads the joining: when the mention of the Kaʿbah and the qiblah had run, that was joined to the mention of Ibrāhīm, and that he is the one who built the House; and it was of the right of the Jews — and they are of the offspring of Ibrāhīm — that they not turn away from his religion. The trial of the verse is given its root: the ibtilāʾ is the testing and the examination, and its meaning is the command and the worship.

**THE WORDS**

The words of the verse are given their faces in the reports. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿIkrimah: no one was tried, with this religion, and established it, except Ibrāhīm; and he said, in his other report: no one was tried with this religion, and stood with all of it, other than Ibrāhīm — he was tried with the Islam and completed it, and God wrote for him the clearing, and He said: and Ibrāhīm who fulfilled; and he mentioned ten in Barāʾah — (9:112 — **“who repent, who are devoted to worship, who praise ˹their Lord˺, who fast, who bow down and prostrate themselves”**); and ten in al-Aḥzāb, in the verse of the Muslim men and the Muslim women and the believers; and ten in the sūrah of the Believers, until the guarding of the prayers; and ten in Sāʾala Sāʾil. Ṭāwūs carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the ten of the fiṭrah: God tried him with ten things, and they are: the fiṭrah, five in the head — the cutting of the mustache, and the rinsing of the mouth, and the snuffing, and the siwāk, and the parting of the head; and five in the body — the clipping of the nails, and the plucking of the armpit, and the shaving of the pubes, and the circumcision, and the cleaning with water. Qatādah said of He fulfilled them: he delivered them; and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said: he stood with them; and al-Nuʿmān said: he worked by them.

Al-Ṭabarī states the standing of the words: the correctness of the saying, with us, is that God informed His servants that He tested Ibrāhīm His intimate one, with words He revealed to him, and commanded him to work by them, and he fulfilled them, as God reported of him that he did; and it is possible that those words be all of what the ones we mentioned said, and it is possible that they be some of it — because Ibrāhīm was tested, in what reached us, with all of that, and worked by it, and stood in it, in the obedience of God and His command; and it is not permissible for anyone to say that God intended, by the words Ibrāhīm was tried with, one of them in particular, without another, nor all of them, except by an argument that must be submitted to — of a report from the Messenger ﷺ, or a consensus of the proof.

**THE IMĀMATE AND ITS COVENANT**

Mujāhid carries the trial to its dialogue: God said to Ibrāhīm: I am trying you with a matter — and what is it? He said: You will make me an imām for the people! He said: yes. He said: and of my offspring. He said: My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers. He said: You will make the House a place of return for the people. He said: yes. He said: and a sanctuary. He said: yes. He said: and You will make us both Muslims to You, and of our offspring a Muslim nation to You. He said: yes. He said: and You will show us our rites, and turn to us. He said: yes. He said: and You will make this land secure. He said: yes. He said: and You will provide its people with the fruits, whoever of them believes. He said: yes.

The covenant of the verse is read by its faces: ʿAṭāʾ ibn Abī Rabāḥ said: My covenant — My mercy; and al-Suddī said: My prophethood; and it was said: the imāmate; and Mujāhid said of My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers: no wrongdoer is to be obeyed in his wrongdoing. Al-Ṭabarī reads the answer: God means, by His saying, My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers — My covenant that I promised you, of the imāmate, does not reach the wrongdoers of your offspring; and the meaning of the speech is: I do not make, of your offspring, a wrongdoer an imām who is followed in the good. The asking of the father is answered, then, by the exception: the offspring is granted the imāmate, except the wrongdoer of them — and the covenant of the verse is the measure that stands over every offspring, after Ibrāhīm, until the Day.

---

## Verse 2:125

> And ˹remember˺ when We made the Sacred House a centre and a sanctuary for the people ˹saying˺, “˹You may˺ take the standing-place of Abraham as a site of prayer.” And We entrusted Abraham and Ishmael to purify My House for those who circle it, who meditate in it, and who bow and prostrate themselves ˹in prayer˺

**THE HOUSE OF THE RETURN**

The verse gives the House its two standings: the return, and the sanctuary. Al-Qurṭubī reads the first: God made — in the meaning of He turned, for its transitivity to two objects; the House — He means the Kaʿbah; a mathābah — a place of return; one says, he returned, a returning, and a mathābah, and a thuʾūb, and a thawabān. Mujāhid said of His saying, a mathābah for the people: they do not fulfill, from it, a need — meaning, they do not finish of their longing for it, however much they return to it. And He said of His saying, and a sanctuary: its making sacred — the one who enters it is not afraid. Al-Ṭabarī carries from Mujāhid: the place of return for the people — they do not fulfill, from it, a need; and a sanctuary — its making sacred, the one who enters it is not afraid.

**THE MAQĀM**

The command of the verse is given its place. Al-Ṭabarī states the reading: the correctness of the saying and the reading, with us, is, and take, with the kasrah of the khāʾ, upon the meaning of the command of taking the maqām of Ibrāhīm a place of prayer — for the established report from the Messenger of God ﷺ. Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh carries the report: the Messenger of God ﷺ read, and take, of the maqām of Ibrāhīm, a place of prayer — and in the ḥajj report: the Messenger of God ﷺ touched the Corner, and walked quickly three, and walked four, then he advanced to the maqām of Ibrāhīm, and read, and take, of the maqām of Ibrāhīm, a place of prayer — and he made the maqām between him and the House, and prayed two rakʿahs. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the maqām its story: Ibrāhīm built it, and Ismāʿīl handed him the stones, and the two said: our Lord, accept from us, You are the Hearing, the Knowing; and when the building rose, and the old man was too weak to lift the stones, he stood upon a stone — and it is the maqām of Ibrāhīm. The maqām is read by its faces: ʿAṭāʾ said: his maqām is ʿArafah, and al-Muzdalifah, and the jamarāt; and Mujāhid said: the Ḥaram, all of it, is the maqām of Ibrāhīm; and the soundest of the sayings, with al-Qurṭubī, is that it is the stone the people know today, at which they pray the two rakʿahs of the arrival ṭawāf — and this is the saying of Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, and Ibn ʿAbbās, and Qatādah. Al-Ṭabarī closes the reading: if there were not, upon the correctness of what we chose, a report from the Messenger of God ﷺ, the obligatory in the saying would be what we said — for the speech is carried upon its known apparent, until what indicates its contrary comes; and there is no doubt that what is known among the people, by the maqām of Ibrāhīm, is the place of prayer God mentioned. The sūrah of Āl ʿImrān sets the maqām among the signs of the House: (3:97 — **“In it are clear signs and the standing-place of Abraham”**).

**THE PURIFYING**

The verse closes with the entrusting of the two builders: and We commanded Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl — purify My House, for the ones who circle, and the ones who reside, and the ones who bow, and the ones who prostrate. The purifying of the House is commanded to the father and the son, before the prayer of the ones who circle is mentioned — and the ones for whom it is purified are named by their four standings: the circling, and the residing, and the bowing, and the prostrating. The House stands, in the verse, as the verse of the father stands in the verse before: a covenant fulfilled, a command delivered, and a place made pure for the ones who come after.

---

## Verse 2:126

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham said, “My Lord, make this city ˹of Mecca˺ secure and provide fruits to its people—those among them who believe in Allah and the Last Day.” He answered, “As for those who disbelieve, I will let them enjoy themselves for a little while, then I will condemn them to the torment of the Fire. What an evil destination!”

**THE SUPPLICATION OF THE CITY**

The verse gives Ibrāhīm his supplication for the city. Al-Qurṭubī reads: a secure land — He means Makkah; and he supplicated, for his offspring and for other than them, with security and the abundance of the living; and it is reported that when he supplicated with this supplication, God commanded Jibrīl, and he uprooted al-Ṭāʾif from al-Shām, and circumambulated with it around the House, a week — and it was named al-Ṭāʾif by that; and of His saying, and provide its people with the fruits, whoever of them believes: who believes is a substitute, of the part from the whole, from the people. Al-Ṭabarī carries the reading of the security: they said — Makkah, since it was created, is a secure sanctuary, from the punishment of God, and the punishment of the tyrants; and Ibrāhīm did not ask his Lord that He secure it from His punishment and the punishment of the tyrants, but he asked Him that He secure its people from the barren years and the droughts, and that He provide its dweller with the fruits, as his Lord reported of him that he asked, by His saying, and when Ibrāhīm said, my Lord, make this a secure land, and provide its people with the fruits, whoever of them believes.

**THE SANCTUARY OF THE TWO MESSENGERS**

The reports give the sanctuary its two tongues. Abū Shurayḥ al-Khuzāʿī carries the day of the conquest: when Makkah was opened, Khuzāʿah killed a man of Hudhayl, and the Messenger of God ﷺ stood, addressing, and said: O people, God made Makkah sacred, the day He created the heavens and the earth, and it is sacred, by the sanctity of God, until the Day of Resurrection; and it is not lawful, for one who believes in God and the Last Day, to shed blood in it, or to cut a tree of it; and it will not be lawful for anyone after me, and it was not made lawful for me except for this hour, in wrath at its people; and it has returned, upon its state of yesterday; and let the one present inform the one absent; and whoever says, the Messenger of God ﷺ killed in it — say, God made it lawful for His Messenger, and He did not make it lawful for you. Ibn ʿAbbās carries the like: the Messenger of God ﷺ said, to Makkah, when he opened it: this is a sanctuary God made sacred, the day He created the heavens and the earth, and created the sun and the moon, and placed these two mountains — it was not lawful for anyone before me, and it will not be lawful for anyone after me; it was made lawful for me, for an hour of a day. Abū Hurayrah carries the second tongue: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: Ibrāhīm was the servant of God and his intimate friend, and I am the servant of God and His Messenger; and Ibrāhīm made Makkah sacred, and I make Madīnah sacred, what is between its two lava-fields — its thorny trees and its game, and no weapon is carried in it for fighting, and no tree of it is cut, except for the fodder of a camel. Al-Ṭabarī gathers the two tongues: the soundness of the meaning of the two reports has become plain — the report of Abū Shurayḥ, and of Ibn ʿAbbās, from the Prophet ﷺ, that he said, and God made Makkah sacred, the day He created the sun and the moon, and the report of Jābir, and Abū Hurayrah, and Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj, and other than them, that the Prophet ﷺ said, O God, Ibrāhīm made Makkah sacred — and one of the two does not repel the correctness of the meaning of the other, as some of the ignorant supposed.

**THE LITTLE ENJOYMENT**

The answer of the verse to the supplication is given by its exception: Ibrāhīm asked the fruits for the believers of the city, and God answered him that He would provide the disbeliever too — and the providing is followed by the condemning. Qatādah said: it reached us that the Ḥaram was made sacred, in its correspondence, to the Throne; and it reached us that the House descended with Ādam, when he descended; and God said to him: My House descends with you, circumambulated around, as My Throne is circumambulated around; and Ādam circumambulated around it, and whoever was after him, of the believers — until, when the time of the Flood came, God raised it and purified it, and the punishment of the people of the earth did not touch it; and Ibrāhīm followed an effect of it, and built it upon an old foundation that was before it. The sūrah of Ibrāhīm carries the same supplication, of the settling before the building: (14:37 — **“Our Lord! I have settled some of my offspring in a barren valley, near Your Sacred House”**). The city is thus asked secure, and provided from the fruits, and the disbeliever of it is let enjoy for a little — and the little of the verse is the measure of every enjoyment that stands outside the believing of the supplication.

---

## Verse 2:127

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham raised the foundation of the House with Ishmael, ˹both praying,˺ “Our Lord! Accept ˹this˺ from us. You are indeed the All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**THE RAISING AND ITS BEGINNING**

The verse gives the building its scene, and the reports give the scene its beginning. Ibn ʿAbbās said: the House was placed upon the corners of the water, upon four corners, two thousand years before the world was created, then the earth was spread out from under the House. Kaʿb carries the like: the House was a thing floating upon the water, before God created the earth by forty years, and from it the earth was spread out; and it was reported from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib: Ibrāhīm came from Armenia, and with him the Sakīnah, guiding him to the placing of the House, as the spider places its house; and it was said to him: God says, and when Ibrāhīm raised the foundations of the House — that was after. Ibn Isḥāq carries from Mujāhid and other than one of the people of knowledge: when God showed Ibrāhīm the place of the House, he went out to it, from al-Shām, and with him went Ismāʿīl and his mother Hājar, and Ismāʿīl a small child, suckling; and they were carried upon the Burāq, and with him Jibrīl, guiding him to the place of the House and the landmarks of the Ḥaram; and he did not pass by a village but said, is it this I was commanded, O Jibrīl? And Jibrīl said, go on! — until he brought him Makkah, and it, at that time, was thorny trees of salam and samar, and in it were a people called the ʿAmāliq, outside Makkah and around it, and the House, that day, was a red hillock of clay; and Ibrāhīm said to Jibrīl: is it here I was commanded to place the two? He said: yes.

**THE TWO BUILDERS AND THE SUPPLICATION**

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the building its detail: he — meaning Ibrāhīm — came, and found Ismāʿīl mending arrows, behind Zamzam; and Ibrāhīm said: O Ismāʿīl, God your Lord has commanded me to build Him a house; and Ismāʿīl said to him: then obey your Lord, in what He commanded you; and Ibrāhīm said to him: He has commanded you to help me upon it; and he said: then I will do it; and he stood with him, and Ibrāhīm built it, and Ismāʿīl handed him the stones, and the two said: our Lord, accept from us, You are the Hearing, the Knowing; and when the building rose, and the old man was too weak to lift the stones, he stood upon a stone — and it is the maqām of Ibrāhīm — and he kept handing him, and the two said: our Lord, accept from us. Al-Qurṭubī reads the word: the foundations — its base, in the saying of Abū ʿUbaydah and al-Farrāʾ; and al-Kisāʾī said: they are the walls; and the known is that they are the base. Al-Ṭabarī gives the supplication its people: we said what we said of that, for the agreement of all the people of interpretation, upon that Ismāʿīl is intended, by the report God reported of him and his father, of what the two used to say — and that is their saying, our Lord, accept from us, You are the Hearing, the Knowing; and it is known that Ismāʿīl would not have said that, except while he was a complete man, or a boy who understood the places of the harm from the benefit, and the obligations of God and His rulings had become binding upon him. The sūrah of Āl ʿImrān states the standing of the House the two built: (3:96 — **“Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah”**). The raising of the verse is thus a raising of the foundations, and a raising of the supplication with it — and the acceptance the two asked is the acceptance that stands, in the sūrah, over every deed of the two.

---

## Verse 2:128

> Our Lord! Make us both ˹fully˺ submit to You and from our descendants a nation that will submit to you. Show us our rituals, and turn to us in grace. You are truly the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**THE ASKING OF THE RITES**

The verse carries the second supplication of the two builders, and the asking of the rites is given its faces. Al-Ṭabarī reads the reading: some of the reciters read it, and show us our rites, in the meaning of the seeing of the eye — show them to our eyes, until we see them; and that is the reading of the generality of the people of al-Ḥijāz and al-Kūfah. The rites are read by two sayings. Al-Suddī carries the first: when Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl finished from the building of the House, God commanded him to call out, and he said, and proclaim to the people the ḥajj; and he called out, between the two mountains of Makkah: O people, God commands you to perform the ḥajj of His House! And it settled in the heart of every believer, and every one who heard of it, of mountain or tree or beast, answered: at Your service, at Your service! — and they answered with the talbiyah: at Your service, O God, at Your service; and God commanded him to go out to ʿArafāt, and described it to him, and he went out; and when he reached the tree, at the pass, the Devil met him, and he stoned him with seven pebbles, saying God is great with every pebble, and he flew, and alighted at the second pillar, and he repelled him, and he stoned him and said God is great, and he flew, and alighted at the third pillar. And others said, of the ones who read this reading: the rites are the places of sacrifice — and the interpretation of the verse, upon the saying of the one who said that, is: show us how to perform, for You, our rites, that we may sacrifice them to You; ʿAṭāʾ said: our slaughtering; and in his other report: our places of sacrifice; and Mujāhid said the like; and ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmayr said: show us our places of sacrifice. ʿAṭāʾ carries the third face: bring them out for us, teach them to us. And it is mentioned that it is in the reading of Ibn Masʿūd: and show them their rites — meaning, show our Muslim offspring their rites. The sūrah of the Ḥajj gives the rites their fruit: (22:28 — **“so they may obtain the benefits ˹in store˺ for them”**).

**THE TURNING**

The verse closes with the turning, and the turning is given its root: as for the tawbah, its root is the returning, from a disliked thing, to a beloved; and the tawbah of the servant to his Lord is his returning, from what God dislikes of him, by the remorse upon it, and the leaving of it, and the resolution of the not returning to it. The two builders ask the showing of the rites, and then the turning upon them — and the asking of the turning, from the two who fulfilled the words, is the sign that the standing of the tawbah is not the standing of the sinner alone: it is the return of the servant to his Lord, in every standing, and the two builders asked it, at the top of their building. The verse is thus the supplication of the rites and the nation and the turning — the three askings of the father and the son, for themselves, and for the nation that will come after them, and for the return that holds them all.

---

## Verse 2:129

> Our Lord! Raise from among them a messenger who will recite to them Your revelations, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them. Indeed, You ˹alone˺ are the Almighty, All-Wise.”

**THE SUPPLICATION ANSWERED BY ITS NAME**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the verse its standing in the sūrah: this is the supplication of Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl, for our Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ in particular, and it is the supplication our Prophet ﷺ used to say of: I am the supplication of my father Ibrāhīm, and the good news of ʿĪsā. Khālid ibn Maʿdān al-Kalāʿī carries the saying: a group of the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ said: O Messenger of God, inform us of yourself; and he said: yes — I am the supplication of my father Ibrāhīm, and the good news of ʿĪsā. Al-ʿIrbāḍ ibn Sāriyah al-Sulamī carries the fuller saying: I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: I am, with God, in the Mother of the Book, the seal of the prophets — while Ādam was still kneaded in his clay; and I will inform you of the interpretation of that: I am the supplication of my father Ibrāhīm, and the good news of ʿĪsā to his people, and the dream of my mother. Al-Suddī said of His saying, our Lord, and raise up, among them, a messenger from among themselves: he is Muḥammad ﷺ; and al-Rabīʿ said: he is Muḥammad ﷺ, and it was said to him: that has been answered, and he is in the last of the time. The supplication of the two builders is thus named, by the one it was answered in, as his own origin: he is the supplication of the father, raised up at the end of the time.

**THE BOOK AND THE WISDOM**

The three deeds of the messenger of the supplication are read one by one. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, of he recites to them Your signs, and purifies them: he means, by the purification, the obedience of God, and the sincerity. Ibn Zayd said of the wisdom: the wisdom is the religion they do not know except by him ﷺ, he teaches them it; and the wisdom is the intelligence in the religion; and the wisdom is a thing God places in the heart, by which He gives it light. Al-Qurṭubī carries the sayings: the Book is the Qurʾān; and the wisdom — the knowledge of the religion, and the understanding in the interpretation, and the understanding that is a disposition and a light from God Most High — Mālik said it, and Ibn Wahb reported it from him, and Ibn Zayd said it; and Qatādah said: the wisdom is the Sunnah, and the making plain of the laws.
